Lifting Coalgebra Modalities and 𝖬𝖤𝖫𝖫 \mathsf{MELL} Model Structure to Eilenberg-Moore Categories Thanks: The author would like to thank Kellogg College, the Clarendon Fund, and the Oxford-Google DeepMind Graduate Scholarship for financial support.
Jean-Simon Pacaud Lemay

Abstract
. A categorical model of the multiplicative and exponential fragments of intuitionistic linear logic ( 𝖬𝖤𝖫𝖫 \mathsf{MELL} ), known as a linear category , is a symmetric monoidal closed category with a monoidal coalgebra modality (also known as a linear exponential comonad). Inspired by Blute and Scott’s work on categories of modules of Hopf algebras as models of linear logic, we study categories of algebras of monads (also known as Eilenberg-Moore categories) as models of 𝖬𝖤𝖫𝖫 \mathsf{MELL} . We define a 𝖬𝖤𝖫𝖫 \mathsf{MELL} lifting monad on a linear category as a Hopf monad – in the Bruguières, Lack, and Virelizier sense – with a special kind of mixed distributive law over the monoidal coalgebra modality. As our main result, we show that the linear category structure lifts to the category of algebras of 𝖬𝖤𝖫𝖫 \mathsf{MELL} lifting monads. We explain how groups in the category of coalgebras of the monoidal coalgebra modality induce 𝖬𝖤𝖫𝖫 \mathsf{MELL} lifting monads and provide a source for such groups from enrichment over abelian groups. Along the way we also define mixed distributive laws of symmetric comonoidal monads over symmetric monoidal comonads and lifting differential category structure.



May 01, 2018Nov. 07, 2019

†
†
lmcs-doi:
Volume 15, Issue 4, Paper 8

Acknowledgements:

The author would first like to thank the reviewers of LMCS for their very useful editorial comments and suggestions for this paper, as well as the reviewers of FSCD for their comments on the extended abstract version of this paper
[
Lem18
]
. The author would also like to thank Jamie Vicary for useful discussions, editorial comments, and suggestions.

1.
Introduction

Linear logic, as introduced by Girard
[
Gir87
]
, is a resource-sensitive logic which due to its flexibility admits multiple different fragments and a wide range of applications. A categorical model of the multiplicative fragment of intuitionistic linear logic (
\mathsf{MILL}
)
[
Bie95
,
GL87
]
is a symmetric monoidal closed category. Categories of modules of Hopf algebras
[
Maj00
,
Swe69
]
(over a commutative ring) are important and of interest especially in representation theory
[
Wis75
]
due in part as they are monoidal closed categories
[
BLV11
,
Maj00
]
. Blute
[
Blu96
]
and Scott
[
BS04
]
studied the idea of interpreting categories of modules of Hopf algebras as models of
\mathsf{MILL}
with negation and its non-commutative variant. If one looks at the more general setting, categories of modules of cocommutative Hopf monoids in arbitrary symmetric monoidal closed categories are again symmetric monoidal closed categories and therefore models of
\mathsf{MILL}
. But for what kind of monoids in categorical models of the multiplicative and exponential fragments of intuitionistic linear logic (
\mathsf{MELL}
), is their categories of modules again a categorical model of
\mathsf{MELL}
?

The exponential fragment of
\mathsf{MELL}
adds in the exponential modality which is a unary connective
\oc
, read as either “of course” or “bang”, admitting four structural rules
[
Bie95
,
Mel09
]
: promotion, dereliction, contraction, and weakening. In terms of the categorical semantics, the exponential modality
\oc
is interpreted as a monoidal coalgebra modality
[
BCS15
]
(see Definition
4.2
), also known as a linear exponential comonad
[
Sch04
]
, which in particular is a symmetric monoidal comonad (see Definition
3.5
), capturing the promotion and dereliction rules, such that for each object
A
, the exponential
\oc(A)
comes equipped with a natural cocommutative comonoid structure (see Definition
4.1
), capturing the contraction and weakening rules. Categorical models of
\mathsf{MELL}
are known as linear categories
[
Bie95
,
Mel03
,
Mel09
]
, which are symmetric monoidal closed categories with monoidal coalgebra modalities. We can now restate the question we seek to answer in this paper:

•

Question 1:
“For what kind of monoid
A
in a linear category, is the category of modules of
A
also a linear category?”

Part of the answer regarding the symmetric monoidal closed structure of a linear category is already known: the monoid
A
needs to be a cocommutative Hopf monoid (see Definition
13
). What remains to be answered is how to ‘extend’, or better yet ‘lift’, the monoidal coalgebra modality to the category of modules of
A
. By ‘lifting’ we mean that the forgetful functor from the Eilenberg-Moore category to base category preserves the required structure strictly. Observing that if
A
is a monoid, then the endofunctor
A\otimes-
is a monad (see Example
3.1
) and so the category of modules of
A
corresponds precisely to the Eilenberg-Moore category of the monad
A\otimes-
. Therefore, we can further generalize the question we want to be answered:

•

Question 2:
“For what kind of monad on a linear category, is the Eilenberg-Moore category of algebras of that monad also a linear category?”

This now becomes a question of how to lift a comonad to the Eilenberg-Moore category of a monad and this brings us into the realm of distributive laws
[
Bec69
,
Wis08
]
. Specifically, one requires a natural transformation known as a mixed distributive law
[
HHM07
,
Wis08
]
(see Definition
5.1
) to obtain a lifting of a comonad to the Eilenberg-Moore category of a monad. In order to lift the symmetric monoidal closed structure of a linear category, the monad in question is required to be a symmetric Hopf monad (see Definition
3.3
) in the sense of Bruguières, Lack, and Virelizier
[
BLV11
,
BV07
]
, which are the monad analogue of cocommutative Hopf monoids (see Example
3.3
). Finally to lift the monoidal coalgebra modality of a linear category the Eilenberg-Moore category of a symmetric Hopf monad one requires a symmetric monoidal mixed distributive law (Definition
5.2
), which is a special kind of mixed distributive law which we introduce in this paper.

1.1.
Main Definitions and Results:

The main new technical definition of this paper is that of a symmetric monoidal mixed distributive law (Definition
5.2
) of a symmetric comonoidal monad over a symmetric monoidal comonad. Symmetric monoidal mixed distributive laws are mixed distributive laws between the underlying monads and comonads but with extra coherences regarding the symmetric (co)monoidal endofunctor structure. Proposition
3
shows that symmetric monoidal mixed distributive laws are in bijective correspondence to liftings of the symmetric monoidal comonad to the Eilenberg-Moore of the symmetric comonoidal monad and vice-versa, which partially answers Question 2. Proposition
4
studies when a symmetric comonoidal monad of the form
A\otimes-
admits a symmetric mixed distributive law over a symmetric monoidal comonad, which is related to answering Question 1, and proves that
A
must be a cocommutative bimonoid in the Eilenberg-Moore category of the comonad. Two new definitions regarding the answer of Question 2 are that of exponential lifting monads (Definition
6.2
) and
\mathsf{MELL}
lifting monads (Definition
6.3
). An exponential lifting monad is a symmetric comonoidal monad with a symmetric monoidal mixed distributive law over a monoidal coalgebra modality, while a
\mathsf{MELL}
lifting monad is an exponential lifting monad on a linear category which is also a symmetric Hopf monad. Proposition
9
provides a partial answer to Question 2, while Theorem
11
provides the full answer. We summarize these two main results as follows:

•

The Eilenberg-Moore category of an exponential lifting monad admits a monoidal coalgebra modality (Proposition
9
).

•

The Eilenberg-Moore category of a
\mathsf{MELL}
lifting monad is a linear category (Theorem
11
).

Proposition
10
and Theorem
12
are dedicated to answering Question 1, which we summarize as (by abusing notation slightly):

•

Monoids
A
in the Eilenberg-Moore category of a monoidal coalgebra modality induce exponential lifting monads
A\otimes-
, and therefore the category of modules over
A
admits a monoidal coalgebra modality (Proposition
10
).

•

Groups
H
in the Eilenberg-Moore category of a monoidal coalgebra modality of a linear category induce
\mathsf{MELL}
lifting monads
H\otimes-
and therefore the category of modules over
H
is a linear category (Theorem
12
).

We note that since the Eilenberg-Moore category of a monoidal coalgebra modality in a Cartesian monoidal category
[
Sch04
]
, it follows that groups in the Eilenberg-Moore category are in fact also cocommutative Hopf monoids in the base category (see Definition
2.1
and Definition
2.3
for more details). We also address the special case of Lafont categories
[
Mel09
]
, that is when the monoidal coalgebra modality is, in fact, a free exponential modality
[
MTT17
,
Sla17
]
. In this case, Corollary
13
states that the category of modules over a cocommutative Hopf monoid is again a Lafont category. In the process of studying liftings of monoidal coalgebra modalities, we also discuss liftings of the strictly weaker notion of coalgebra modalities (Definition
6.1
) in order to also discuss lifting differential category structure (Definition
7.4
). In discussing differential categories, we also explain how additive structure and negatives provide a source of the desired monoids for answering Question 2 (Proposition
14
and Theorem
17
).

1.2.
Organization of this paper

Section
2
is dedicated to reviewing the familiar concepts of symmetric monoidal categories, (co)monoids, bimonoids, and Hopf monoids. Section
3
reviews the notions of (co)monads, symmetric comonoidal monads, symmetric monoidal comonads, and Hopf monads, where in particular we also discuss in detail the structures on their Eilenberg-Moore categories. In Section
4
we review the categorical semantics of
\mathsf{MELL}
by defining coalgebra modalities, monoidal coalgebra modalities, linear categories, and Lafont categories. Section
5
begins by reviewing the notion of a mixed distributive law and later also introduces and studies symmetric monoidal mixed distributive laws. Section
6
is dedicated to answering Question 1 and Question 2 by introducing exponential lifting monads and
\mathsf{MELL}
lifting monads and stating the main results of this paper. Finally, Section
7
addresses lifting differential category structure and how additive structure induces a source of exponential lifting monads and
\mathsf{MELL}
lifting monads.

2.
Monoids, Comonoids, Bimonoids, and Hopf Monoids

In this section, we review the notions of monoids, comonoids, bimonoids, and Hopf monoids. We refer the reader to
[
Kel64
,
Mel09
]
for a more detailed introduction to monoids and comonoids, and to
[
BS04
,
Blu96
]
for details on bimonoids and Hopf monoids. We conclude this section with examples of Hopf monoids (and therefore also examples of monoids, comonoids, and bimonoids) in various examples of well known symmetric monoidal closed categories (Example
2.3
).

2.1.
Symmetric Monoidal (Closed) Categories

We begin by first recalling the definitions of symmetric monoidal categories, Cartesian monoidal categories, and symmetric monoidal closed categories. The axiomatization of these concepts can be given in various equivalent ways. In this paper, we have chosen to express them as found in
[
Mel09
]
. For a more in-depth introduction to symmetric monoidal categories, we refer to reader to the following introductory sources
[
BW90
,
ML13
,
Mel09
]

{defi}

A
symmetric monoidal category

[
Mel09
]
is a septuple

(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K,\alpha,\ell,\rho,\sigma)

consisting of a category
\mathbb{X}
, a functor
\otimes:\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
called the
monoidal product
, an object
K
of
\mathbb{X}
called the
monoidal unit
, and four natural isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\alpha_{A,B,C}

\displaystyle:A\otimes(B\otimes C)\xrightarrow{\cong}(A\otimes B)\otimes C

\displaystyle\ell_{A}

\displaystyle:K\otimes A\xrightarrow{\cong}A

\displaystyle\sigma_{A,B}

\displaystyle:A\otimes B\xrightarrow{\cong}B\otimes A

\displaystyle\rho_{A}

\displaystyle:A\otimes K\xrightarrow{\cong}A

such that the following diagrams commute:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 16.90448pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-16.90448pt\raise (1)

There are several notions of “morphisms” of symmetric monoidal categories, that is, functors between symmetric monoidal categories which are compatible with the symmetric monoidal structures in a certain sense. In this paper we discuss two such as examples: symmetric comonoidal functors (Definition
3.2
.i) and symmetric monoidal functors (Definition
3.5
.i). From now on (to avoid overloading definitions with notation), symmetric monoidal categories will simply be denoted as triples
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
when there is no confusion about the natural isomorphisms
\alpha
,
\ell
,
\rho
, and
\sigma
. This should hopefully not cause any confusion as this paper only works with symmetric monoidal categories and none of the weaker alternatives (such as monoidal categories or braided monoidal categories
[
Mel09
]
).

A particularly useful natural isomorphism in a symmetric monoidal category is the
interchange map

\tau_{A,B,C,D}:(A\otimes B)\otimes(C\otimes D)\xrightarrow{\cong}(A\otimes C)\otimes(B\otimes D)
which is defined as the following composite:

(2)

The interchange map appears in the coherences of a bimonoid (Definition
2.3
) and of a monoidal coalgebra modality (Definition
4.2
).

Special examples of symmetric monoidal categories are those whose monoidal structure is, in fact, a finite product structure.

{defi}

A
Cartesian monoidal category

[
Mel09
]
is a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
such that
K
is a
terminal object

[
ML13
]
, that is, for each object
A
there exists a unique map
{\mathsf{t}_{A}:A\to K}
; and
A\otimes B
is a
product

[
ML13
]
of
A
and
B
with projection maps:

\textstyle{A\otimes B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{A}\otimes\mathsf{t}_{B}}
\textstyle{A\otimes K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{A}}
\textstyle{A}

\textstyle{A\otimes B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{t}_{B}\otimes 1_{B}}
\textstyle{K\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ell_{B}}
\textstyle{B}

that is, for every pair of maps
{f:C\to A}
and
g:C\to B
, there exists a
unique
map
{\langle f,g\rangle:C\to A\otimes B}
such that the following diagram commutes:

350.41992pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(3)

The map
\langle f,g\rangle
is called the pairing of
f
and
g
.

Any category with chosen products and terminal object is a Cartesian symmetric monoidal category where the natural isomorphisms
\alpha
,
\ell
,
\rho
, and
\sigma
are all induced by the universal property of the product
[
Sel10
]
. It is also worth mentioning that a category with chosen coproducts is also a symmetric monoidal category and therefore its opposite category is a Cartesian monoidal category.

{defi}

A
symmetric monoidal closed category

[
Mel09
]
is a symmetric mon- oidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
such that for each pair of objects
A
and
B
, there is an object
A\multimap B
, called the
internal hom
of
A
and
B
, and a map
\mathsf{ev}_{A,B}:(A\multimap B)\otimes A\to B
, called the
evaluation map
, such that for every map
f:C\otimes A\to B
, there exists a
unique
map
\overline{f}:C\to A\multimap B
such that the following diagram commutes:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 33.17006pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-16.79231pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-11.00002pt\raise-14.99998pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{f}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-23.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.63914pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 93.17006pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 93.17006pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-33.17006pt\raise-31.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 47.74783pt\raise-18.49205pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.56667pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{~~~\mathsf{ev}_{A,B}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 93.17006pt\raise-2.22856pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(4)

A
Cartesian closed category

[
BW90
]
is a Cartesian monoidal category which is also a symmetric monoidal closed category.

{exas}

Here are some well-known examples of symmetric monoidal closed categories:

(1)

Let
\mathsf{SET}
be the category of sets and functions between them. Then
(\mathsf{SET},\times,\{\ast\})
is a Cartesian monoidal category where the product is given by the Cartesian product of sets
\times
, the terminal object is a chosen singleton
\{\ast\}
.
(\mathsf{SET},\times,\{\ast\})
is also a Cartesian closed category where in particular
X\multimap Y
is the set of all functions from
X
to
Y
.

(2)

Let
\mathsf{REL}
be the category of sets and relations, where recall that a relation from a set
X
to a set
Y
, denoted
R:X\to Y
, is a subset
R\subseteq X\times Y
. Then
(\mathsf{REL},\times,\{\ast\})
is a symmetric monoidal category where the monoidal product and monoidal unit are again given by the Cartesian product of sets and a chosen singleton respectively. It is important to note that the Cartesian product of sets is not a product (in the categorical sense) for
\mathsf{REL}
, and therefore
(\mathsf{REL},\times,\{\ast\})
is not a Cartesian monoidal category.
(\mathsf{REL},\times,\{\ast\})
is also a symmetric monoidal closed category with
X\multimap Y:=X\times Y
.

(3)

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a field and
\mathsf{VEC}_{\mathbb{K}}
the category of
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces and
\mathbb{K}
-linear maps between them. Then
(\mathsf{VEC}_{\mathbb{K}},\otimes,\mathbb{K})
is a symmetric monoidal closed category where the monoidal product is given by the algebraic tensor product of vector spaces
\otimes
, and the monoidal unit is the field
\mathbb{K}
.
(\mathsf{VEC}_{\mathbb{K}},\otimes,\mathbb{K})
is also symmetric monoidal closed category where
V\multimap W
is the
\mathbb{K}
-vector space of all
\mathbb{K}
-linear maps from
V
to
W
.

2.2.
Monoids and Comonoids

Here we review the notion of monoids and comonoids. While monoids and comonoids are dual notions (in the categorical sense), we take the pain of defining both concepts explicitly.

{defi}

Let
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
be a symmetric monoidal category. A

Mel09

in
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)

consisting of an object

called the
multiplication

\mathsf{u}:K\to A
called the
unit

(7)

{defi}

Let
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
be a symmetric monoidal category. A
comonoid

[
Mel09
]
in
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
is a triple
(C,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
consisting of an object
C
, a map
\Delta:C\to C\otimes C
called the
comultiplication
, and a map
\mathsf{e}:C\to K
called the
counit
such that the following diagrams commute:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern (10)

Every object of a Cartesian monoidal category comes equipped with a unique cocommutative comonoid structure using the diagonal map and terminal map. In the categorical semantics of linear logic, this is of particular interests when considering the Eilenberg-Moore category of a monoidal coalgebra modality (Section
4.2
).

{lemC}

[
[
Por08
]
] Let
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
be a Cartesian monoidal category. Then every object
A
admits a unique cocommutative comonoid structure
(A,\Delta_{A},\mathsf{t}_{A})
where the counit
\mathsf{t}_{A}:A\to K
is the unique map to the terminal object
K
, and
\Delta_{A}:=\langle 1_{A},1_{A}\rangle
, that is, the unique map which makes the following diagram commute:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 81.21524pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 171.54156pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 178.29156pt\raise-14.75pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.92778pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\exists\oc\Delta_{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 178.29156pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.17313pt\raise 0.9849pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern-0.17313pt\raise-0.9849pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 264.43735pt\raise-15.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.17313pt\raise (11)

Furthermore, for every map
f:A\to B
,
f:(A,\Delta_{A},\mathsf{t}_{A})\to(B,\Delta_{B},\mathsf{t}_{B})
is a comonoid morphism.

2.3.
Bimonoids and Hopf Monoids

Bimonoids are both monoids and comonoids such that monoid and comonoid structures are compatible.

{defi}

In a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
, a
bimonoid

[
Por08
]
is a quintuple
(A,\nabla,\mathsf{u},\Delta,\mathsf{e})
such that
(A,\nabla,\mathsf{u})
is a monoid,
(A,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
is a comonoid, and the following diagrams commute:

(12)

where
\tau
is the interchange map as defined in (
2
). A bimonoid is said to be (co)commutative if the underlying (co)monoid is (co)commutative.

For a Cartesian monoidal category, every monoid induces a cocommutative bimonoid. This is because the bimonoid identities are equivalent to requiring that the multiplication and unit be comonoid morphisms, and dually that the comultiplication and counit be monoid morphisms.

{lemC}

[
[
Por08
]
] Let
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
be a Cartesian monoidal category and
(A,\nabla,\mathsf{u})
a monoid in
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
. Then
(A,\nabla,\mathsf{u},\Delta_{A},\mathsf{t}_{A})
is a cocommutative bimonoid, where
(A,\Delta_{A},\mathsf{t}_{A})
is the unique cocommutative comonoid structure on
A
from Lemma
2.2
.

{defi}

In a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
, a
Hopf monoid

[
BS04
,
Blu96
]
is a sextuple
(H,\nabla,\mathsf{u},\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{S})
consisting of a bimonoid
(H,\nabla,\mathsf{u},\Delta,\mathsf{e})
and a map
\mathsf{S}:H\to H
called the antipode such that the following diagram commutes:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 31.56248pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H\otimes 89.5762pt\raise 5.84723pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.01389pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{1\otimes\mathsf{S}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 131.24287pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern (13)

A Hopf monoid is said to be (co)commutative if its underlying bimonoid is (co)commutative.

Here are a few well known properties about Hopf monoids:

{lemC}

[
[
Blu96
,
Maj00
]
] Let
(H,\nabla,\mathsf{u},\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{S})
be a Hopf monoid in a symmetric monoidal category.

(1)

The antipode is unique, that is, if
(H,\nabla,\mathsf{u},\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{S}^{\prime})
is also a Hopf monoid then
\mathsf{S}=\mathsf{S}^{\prime}
.

(2)

If
(H,\nabla,\mathsf{u},\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{S})
is commutative (resp. cocommutative) then the antipode
\mathsf{S}:(H,\nabla,\mathsf{u})\to(H,\nabla,\mathsf{u})
is a monoid morphism (resp.
\mathsf{S}:(H,\Delta,\mathsf{e})\to(H,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
is a comonoid morphism).

In Cartesian monoidal categories, Hopf monoids are better known as groups:

{defi}

In a Cartesian monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
, a
(abelian) group

[
Has10
]
is a quadruple
(G,\nabla,\mathsf{u},\mathsf{S})
consisting of a (commutative) monoid
(G,\nabla,\mathsf{u})
and a map
\mathsf{S}:G\to G
such that
(G,\nabla,\mathsf{u},\Delta_{A},\mathsf{t}_{A},\mathsf{S})
is a (commutative and) cocommutative Hopf monoid, where
(A,\Delta_{A},\mathsf{t}_{A})
is the unique cocommutative comonoid structure on
A
from Lemma
2.2
.

{exas}

As cocommutative Hopf monoids will be of particular interest for this paper, we conclude this section with some examples of Hopf monoids in the symmetric monoidal categories from Example
2.1
.

(1)

Since
(\mathsf{SET},\times,\{\ast\})
is a Cartesian monoidal category, by Lemma
2.2
, every set admits a unique cocommutative comonoid structure. Monoids in
(\mathsf{SET},\times,\{\ast\})
coincide with the classical notion of monoids, and by Lemma
2.3
are therefore cocommutative bimonoids. Similarly, groups in
(\mathsf{SET},\times,\{\ast\})
correspond to the classical notion of groups. Explicitly if
G
is a group in the classical sense with binary operation
\cdot
, unit element
u
, and inverse operation
(-)^{-1}
, then
(G,\nabla,\mathsf{u},\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{S})
is a cocommutative Hopf monoid in
(\mathsf{SET},\times,\{\ast\})
where:

\textstyle{G\times G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nabla}
\textstyle{G}
\textstyle{\{\ast\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{u}}
\textstyle{G}
\textstyle{(g,h)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{g\cdot h}
\textstyle{\ast\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{u}

\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta}
\textstyle{G\times G}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{e}}
\textstyle{\{\ast\}}
\textstyle{g\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(g,g)}
\textstyle{g\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ast}

\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{S}}
\textstyle{G}
\textstyle{g\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{g^{-1}}

(2)

Monoids in
(\mathsf{REL},\times,\{\ast\})
are studied in
[
JJ17
]
, while bimonoids and Hopf monoids in
(\mathsf{REL},\times,\{\ast\})
are studied in
[
Has10
]
. In particular, every group in
(\mathsf{SET},\times,\{\ast\})
induces a cocommutative Hopf monoid in
(\mathsf{REL},\times,\{\ast\})
. Explicitly, if
G
is a group (in the classical sense with binary operation, unit, and inverse operation as in the previous example), then the following relations provide a cocommutative Hopf monoid structure on
G
in
(\mathsf{REL},\times,\{\ast\})
:

\nabla:=\{\left((g,h),g\cdot h|~g,h\in G\right)\}\subseteq(G\times G)\times G\quad\quad\mathsf{u}:=\{(\ast,u)\}\subseteq\{\ast\}\times G

\Delta:=\{\left(g,(g,g)\right)\}\subseteq G\times(G\times G)\quad\quad\quad\mathsf{e}:=\{(g,\ast)|~g\in G\}\subseteq G\times\{\ast\}

\mathsf{S}:=\{(g,g^{-1})|~g\in G\}\subseteq G\times G

Note that since
(\mathsf{REL},\times,\{\ast\})
is not a Cartesian monoidal category, there are examples of comonoids in
(\mathsf{REL},\times,\{\ast\})
whose comultiplication and counit are not given as the diagonal relation or terminal relation as above. See Example
4.2
.2 for such an example.

(3)

Monoids, comonoids, bimonoids, and Hopf monoids in
(\mathsf{VEC}_{\mathbb{K}},\otimes,\mathbb{K})
are more commonly referred to as
\mathbb{K}
-algebras,
\mathbb{K}
-coalgebras,
\mathbb{K}
-bialgebras, and
\mathbb{K}
-Hopf algebras respectively. A simple example of a cocommutative
\mathbb{K}
-Hopf algebra are group
\mathbb{K}
-algebras
[
Maj00
]
. Let
G
be a group (again in the classical sense) and let
\mathbb{K}[G]
be the free
\mathbb{K}
-vector space over the set
G
, where we denote the basis elements of
\mathbb{K}[G]
as
e_{g}
for all
g\in G
.
\mathbb{K}[G]
is known as the group
\mathbb{K}
-algebra and it is a cocommutative
\mathbb{K}
-Hopf algebra with the following
\mathbb{K}
-linear maps (which are defined on the basis elements):

\textstyle{\mathbb{K}[G]\otimes\mathbb{K}[G]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nabla}
\textstyle{\mathbb{K}[G]}
\textstyle{\mathbb{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{u}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{K}[G]}
\textstyle{e_{g}\otimes e_{h}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{e_{g\cdot h}}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{e_{u}}

\textstyle{\mathbb{K}[G]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{K}[G]\otimes\mathbb{K}[G]}
\textstyle{\mathbb{K}[G]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{e}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{K}}
\textstyle{e_{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{e_{g}\otimes e_{g}}
\textstyle{e_{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}

\textstyle{\mathbb{K}[G]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{S}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{K}[G]}
\textstyle{e_{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{e_{g^{-1}}}

Other examples of
\mathbb{K}
-Hopf algebras include the polynomial rings
\mathbb{K}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
, the tensor algebra
\mathsf{T}(V)
and the symmetric algebra
\mathsf{S}(V)
over a
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces
V
. For more details on these examples and other examples of Hopf algebras, see
[
BS04
,
Blu96
,
Maj00
,
Swe69
]
.

3.
Monads and Comonads on Symmetric Monoidal Categories

In this section, we review the notion of monads and comonads and their respective Eilenberg-Moore categories. In this paper, we are particularly interested in monads and comonads on symmetric monoidal categories whose Eilenberg-Moore category is again a symmetric monoidal category such that the forgetful functor preserves the symmetric monoidal structure strictly. These special monads and comonads are known as symmetric comonoidal monads and symmetric monoidal comonads respectively. Again while these concepts are dual notions, we define both in detail to provide a complete story and to introduce notion. We also discuss Hopf monads, in the sense of Bruguières, Lack, and Virelizier, in order to lift symmetric monoidal closed structure. We refer the reader to
[
ML13
]
for a detailed introduction of monads and comonads, and to
[
BLV11
,
BV07
,
Moe02
]
for more details on comonoidal monads and Hopf monads, and to
[
Bie95
,
HS03
,
Mel03
,
Mel09
]
for more details on symmetric monoidal comonads.

3.1.
Monads and their Algebras

{defi}

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a category.

\mathbb{X}

and two natural transformations

and

such that the following diagrams commute:

(2)

A
\mathsf{T}
-algebra
is a pair
(A,\nu)
consisting of an object

and a map
\nu:\mathsf{T}(A)\to A
of
\mathbb{X}
such that the following diagrams commute:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern (15)

(3)

For each object
A
, the
free
\mathsf{T}
-algebra
over
A
is the
\mathsf{T}
-algebra
(\mathsf{T}(A),\mu_{A})
.

(4)

A
\mathsf{T}
-algebra morphism

f:(A,\nu)\to(B,\nu^{\prime})
is a map
f:A\to B
such that the following diagrams commute:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern (16)

(5)

The category of
\mathsf{T}
-algebras and
\mathsf{T}
-algebra morphisms is denoted
\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}}
and is called as the
Eilenberg-Moore category of algebras
of the monad
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta)
. There is a forgetful functor
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}:\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}}\to\mathbb{X}
, which is defined on objects as
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}(A,\nu)=A
and on maps as
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}(f)=f
.

A particular source of examples of monads which we will be interested in for this paper are monads on symmetric monoidal category whose endofunctors are of the form
A\otimes-
. Monads of this form are induced by monoids
[
BV07
]
and their algebras are more commonly known as modules.

{exas}

For a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
, every object
A
induces an endofunctor
A\otimes-:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
defined in the obvious way. If
(A,\nabla,\mathsf{u})
is a monoid in
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
, then the triple
(A\otimes-,\mu^{\nabla},\eta^{u})
is a monad on
\mathbb{X}
where the monad structure
\mu^{\nabla}_{X}:A\otimes(A\otimes X)\to A\otimes X
and
\eta_{A}^{\mathsf{u}}:X\to A\otimes X
are defined as follows:

\begin{gathered}\mu^{\nabla}_{X}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 31.14577pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-31.14577pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\otimes(A\otimes X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 45.12784pt\raise 5.49583pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.51807pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\alpha_{A,A,X}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 91.14577pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 91.14577pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(A\otimes A)\otimes X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 168.75494pt\raise 5.89445pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.8889pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\nabla\otimes 1_{X}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 213.4373pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 213.4373pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\otimes X}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\\
\eta^{\mathsf{u}}_{X}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 7.53471pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.53471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.63708pt\raise 6.62279pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.61723pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ell^{-1}_{X}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 67.53471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 67.53471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{K\otimes X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 120.88419pt\raise 5.75833pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75278pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{u}\otimes 1_{X}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 164.03459pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 164.03459pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\otimes X}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(17)

(A\otimes-,\mu,\eta)
is a monad on
\mathbb{X}
, then the triple

is a monoid in
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
where

and
\mathsf{u}^{\eta}:K\to A
are defined as follows:

(18)

In fact, these constructions provide a bijective correspondence between monoid structures on
A
and monad structures on
A\otimes-

[
BV07
]
. For a monoid
(A,\nabla,\mathsf{u})
, the algebras of the induced monad
(A\otimes-,\mu^{\nabla},\eta^{u})
are better known as (left)
(A,\nabla,\mathsf{u})
-modules
[
Bra14
]
. An
(A,\nabla,\mathsf{u})
-module is a pair
(M,\alpha)
consisting of an object
M
and a map
\nu:A\otimes M\to M
satisfying (
15
), that is, the following diagrams commute:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 32.00688pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-32.00688pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\otimes(A\otimes M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-25.92606pt\raise-16.16667pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75278pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{1_{A}\otimes\nu}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.33334pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 45.48029pt\raise 5.49583pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.51807pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\alpha_{A,A,M}}$}}}\kern (19)

The Eilenberg-Moore category of
(A\otimes-,\mu^{\nabla},\eta^{u})
will be denoted as
\mathsf{MOD}(A,\nabla,\mathsf{u}):=\mathbb{X}^{A\otimes-}
.

3.2.
Symmetric Comonoidal Monads

Before giving the definition, we should address the terminology regarding comonoidal monads. Comonoidal monads were originally introduced under the name Hopf monad by Moerdijk
[
Moe02
]
, but are also referred to as
bimonad
in
[
BLV11
,
BV07
]
since bimonoids and Hopf monoids induce examples of such monads. However, in the linear logic community, the terminology for the dual notion is symmetric monoidal comonad
[
Bie95
,
HS03
,
Mel03
,
Mel09
]
. Therefore we have elected to use the same terminology in this paper and thus refer to these monads as symmetric comonoidal monads.

{defi}

Let
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
be a symmetric monoidal category. A
symmetric

comonoidal endofunctor

1
1

1

Comonoidal functors are also known as oplax monoidal functors
[
McC02
]
.

[
McC02
]
on
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
is a triple
(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
consisting of an endofunctor
\mathsf{T}:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
, a natural transformation
\mathsf{n}_{A,B}:\mathsf{T}(A\otimes B)\to\mathsf{T}(A)\otimes\mathsf{T}(B)
, and a map
{\mathsf{n}_{K}:\mathsf{T}(K)\to K}
such that the following diagrams commute:

(20)

A
symmetric comonoidal monad

[
Moe02
]
on
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
is a quintuple
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
consisting of a monad
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta)
and a symmetric comonoidal endofunctor
(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
such that the following diagrams commute:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern (21)

As observed by Moerdijk in
[
Moe02
, Proposition 3.2]
, the Eilenberg-Moore category of a symmetric comonoidal monad is a symmetric monoidal category. In fact for a monad on a symmetric monoidal category, symmetric comonoidal structures on the monad are in bijective correspondence with symmetric monoidal structures on the Eilenberg-Moore category which are strictly preserved by the forgetful functor
[
Wis08
]
. Indeed, let
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
be a symmetric comonoidal monad on a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
. Then
\left(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}},\otimes^{\mathsf{n}},(K,\mathsf{n}_{K}),\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\ell},\tilde{\rho},\tilde{\sigma}\right)
is a symmetric monoidal category. Here the tensor product
\otimes^{\mathsf{n}}
of a pair of
\mathsf{T}
-algebras
(A,\nu)
and
(B,\nu^{\prime})
, is defined as the pair
(A,\nu)\otimes^{\mathsf{n}}(B,\nu^{\prime}):=(A\otimes B,\nu\otimes^{\mathsf{n}}\nu^{\prime})
where
\nu\otimes^{\mathsf{n}}\nu^{\prime}
is defined as follows:

\begin{gathered}\nu\otimes^{\mathsf{n}}\nu^{\prime}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 24.19617pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-24.19617pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{T}(A\otimes B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 42.83133pt\raise 5.54443pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.56667pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{n}_{A,B}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 84.19617pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 84.19617pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{T}(A)\otimes\mathsf{T}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 163.46298pt\raise 6.31447pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.48112pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\nu\otimes\nu^{\prime}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 204.9497pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 204.9497pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\otimes B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(22)

while the tensor product of
\mathsf{T}
-algebra morphisms
f
and
g
is defined as
f\otimes^{\mathsf{n}}g:=f\otimes g
. This tensor product is well defined by the left hand side diagrams of (
21
) and also that
\mathsf{n}
is a
\mathsf{T}
-algebra morphism. While the right hand side diagrams of (
21
) imply that
(K,\mathsf{n}_{K})
is a
\mathsf{T}
-algebras. This is well defined by the two left hand side diagrams of (
21
). The natural isomorphisms
\tilde{\alpha}
,
\tilde{\ell}
,
\tilde{\rho}
and
\tilde{\sigma}
are defined as:

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{(A,\nu),(B,\nu^{\prime}),(C.\nu^{\prime}\prime)}

\displaystyle:=\alpha_{A,B,C}

\displaystyle\tilde{\ell}_{(A,\nu)}

\displaystyle:=\ell_{A}

\displaystyle\tilde{\sigma}_{(A,\nu),(B,\nu^{\prime})}

\displaystyle:=\sigma_{A,B}

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{(A,\nu)}

\displaystyle:=\rho_{A}

which are well defined by the diagrams of (
20
).

As was the case for monads, particular examples of symmetric comonoidal monads of interest for this paper are those whose endofunctors are of the form
A\otimes-
. Symmetric comonoidal monads of this form are induced by cocommutative bimonoids, which is why Bruguières, Lack, and Virelizier refer to comonoidal monads as bimonads
[
BLV11
]
.

{exas}

Let
(A,\nabla,\mathsf{u},\Delta,\mathsf{e})
be a cocommutative bimonoid in a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
. Then
(A\otimes-,\mu^{\nabla},\eta^{\mathsf{u}},\mathsf{n}^{\Delta},\mathsf{n}^{\mathsf{e}}_{K})
is a symmetric comonoidal monad on
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
where the monad
(A\otimes-.\mu^{\nabla},\eta^{\mathsf{u}})
is defined as in Example
3.1
while

\mathsf{n}^{\Delta}_{X,Y}:A\otimes(X\otimes Y)\to(A\otimes X)\otimes(A\otimes Y)
and
\mathsf{n}^{\mathsf{e}}_{K}:A\otimes K\to K
are defined as follows:

\begin{gathered}\mathsf{n}^{\Delta}_{X,Y}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 45.15962pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-31.40965pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\otimes(X\otimes Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 63.4893pt\raise 6.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\Delta\otimes(1_{X}\otimes 1_{Y})}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 147.31924pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 147.31924pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-45.15962pt\raise-14.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(A\otimes A)\otimes(X\otimes Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 56.30856pt\raise-8.50417pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.51807pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\tau_{A,A,X,Y}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 105.15962pt\raise-14.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 105.15962pt\raise-14.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(A\otimes X)\otimes(A\otimes Y)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\\
\\
\mathsf{n}^{\mathsf{e}}_{K}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 17.46524pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-17.46524pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\otimes K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.55043pt\raise 5.1875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\rho_{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 77.46524pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 77.46524pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 116.40968pt\raise 4.55556pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{e}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 150.96524pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 150.96524pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{K}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(23)

where
\tau
is the interchange map as defined in (
2
). Therefore
\left(\mathsf{MOD}(A,\nabla,\mathsf{u}),\otimes^{\mathsf{n}^{\Delta}},(K,\mathsf{n}^{\mathsf{e}}_{K})\right)
is a symmetric monoidal category. Conversly if
(A\otimes-,\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
is a symmetric comonoidal monad on
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
, then
(A,\mathsf{m}^{\mu},\mathsf{u}^{\eta},\Delta^{\mathsf{n}},\mathsf{e}^{n_{K}})
is a cocommutative bimonoid where the monoid
(A,\mathsf{m}^{\mu},\mathsf{u}^{\eta})
is defined as in Example
3.1
while
\Delta^{n}:A\to A\otimes A
and
\mathsf{e}^{n_{K}}:A\to K
are defined as follows:

\begin{gathered}\Delta^{\mathsf{n}}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 74.8802pt\raise 6.80057pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43947pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{1_{A}\otimes\rho^{-1}_{K}}$}}}\kern \mathsf{e}^{n_{K}}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 16.54352pt\raise 6.80057pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43947pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\rho^{-1}_{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 42.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 42.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\otimes K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 87.60475pt\raise 5.05833pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.05278pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{n}_{K}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 113.68048pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 113.68048pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{K}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(24)

These constructions are inverse to each other and gives a bijective correspondence between cocommutative bimonoid structure on
A
and symmetric comonoidal monad structure on
A\otimes-

[
BV07
]
.

3.3.
Symmetric Hopf Monads

We now address how to lift symmetric monoidal closed structure to Eilenberg-Moore categories of symmetric comonoidal monads in such a way that the forgetful functor also preserves the closed structure strictly. For this, we turn to Bruguières, Lack, and Virelizier’s notion of a Hopf monad (which differs to Moerdijk’s definition
[
Moe02
]
). Hopf monads were originally introduced by Bruguières and Virelizier for monoidal categories with duals
[
BV07
]
, but the definition of Hopf monads was later extended to arbitrary monoidal categories by the two previous authors and Lack
[
BLV11
]
. We choose the latter of the two as the definition is somewhat simpler.

{defi}

Let
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
be a symmetric comonoidal monad on a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
. The (left and right)
fusion operators

[
BLV11
]
of
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
are the natural transformations
\mathsf{h}^{l}_{A,B}:\mathsf{T}(A\otimes\mathsf{T}B)\to\mathsf{T}(A)\otimes\mathsf{T}(B)
and
\mathsf{h}^{r}_{A,B}:\mathsf{T}(\mathsf{T}(A)\otimes B)\to\mathsf{T}(A)\otimes\mathsf{T}(B)
defined respectively as:

\mathsf{h}^{l}_{A,\mathsf{T}(B)}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 26.48787pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-26.48787pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{T}(A\otimes\mathsf{T}B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 45.12303pt\raise 5.54443pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.56667pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{n}_{A,B}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 86.48787pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 86.48787pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{T}(A)\otimes\mathsf{T}\mathsf{T}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 170.65744pt\raise 5.93611pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.57501pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{1\otimes\mu_{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 214.04698pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 214.04698pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{T}(A)\otimes\mathsf{T}(B)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

\mathsf{h}^{r}_{A,B}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 31.48785pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-31.48785pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{T}(\mathsf{T}(A)\otimes B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 45.71327pt\raise 5.84999pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.26111pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{n}_{\mathsf{T}(A),B}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 91.48785pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 91.48785pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{T}\mathsf{T}(A)\otimes\mathsf{T}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 175.65742pt\raise 5.93611pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.57501pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mu_{A}\otimes 1}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 219.04697pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 219.04697pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{T}(A)\otimes\mathsf{T}(B)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

A
symmetric Hopf monad

[
BLV11
]
on a symmetric monoidal category is a symmetric comonoidal monad whose fusion operators are natural isomorphisms.

Extending on
[
BLV11
, Theorem 3.6]
, the Eilenberg-Moore category of a Hopf monad on a symmetric monoidal closed category is again a symmetric monoidal closed category such that the forgetful functor preserves the symmetric monoidal closed structure strictly. Explicitly, let
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
be a symmetric Hopf monad on a symmetric monoidal closed category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
, with internal hom
\multimap
and evaluation map
\mathsf{ev}
. Then
\left(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}},\otimes^{\mathsf{n}},(K,\mathsf{n}_{K})\right)
is a symmetric monoidal closed category where the internal hom of a pair of
(A,\nu)
and
(B,\nu^{\prime})
, is defined as the pair
(A,\nu)\multimap(B,\nu^{\prime}):=(A\multimap B,\overline{\gamma}_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}})
where
\overline{\gamma}_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}:\mathsf{T}(A\multimap B)\to A\multimap B
is the currying (as defined in (
4
)) of the map
{\gamma_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}:\mathsf{T}(A\multimap B)\otimes A\to B}
defined as the following composite:

\textstyle{\mathsf{T}(A\multimap B)\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{\mathsf{T}(A\multimap B)}\otimes\eta_{A}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{T}(A\multimap B)\otimes\mathsf{T}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{h}^{l^{-1}}_{A\multimap B,A}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{T}((A\multimap B)\otimes\mathsf{T}(A))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{T}(1_{A\multimap B}\otimes\nu)}
\textstyle{\mathsf{T}((A\multimap B)\otimes A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{T}(\mathsf{ev}_{A,B})}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathsf{T}(B)}
\scriptstyle{\nu^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B}

and with evaluation map
\mathsf{ev}_{(A,\nu),(B,\nu^{\prime})}:=\mathsf{ev}_{A,B}
.

The name Hopf monad comes from the fact that every Hopf monoid (with invertible antipode, which is the case for cocommutative Hopf monoids) defines a Hopf monad whose endofunctor is of the form
H\otimes-
.

{exas}

Let
(H,\nabla,\mathsf{u},\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{S})
be a cocommtuative Hopf monoid in a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
. Then for the induced
(H\otimes-,\mu^{\nabla},\eta^{\mathsf{u}},\mathsf{n}^{\Delta},\mathsf{n}^{\mathsf{e}}_{K})
symmetric comonoidal monad as defined in Example
3.2
, the inverse of the left fusion operator,
\mathsf{h}^{l^{-1}}_{A,B}:(H\otimes A)\otimes(H\otimes B)\to H\otimes\left(A\otimes(H\otimes B)\right)
, is given by the following composite:

\textstyle{(H\otimes A)\otimes(H\otimes B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{H,A,H\otimes B}}
\textstyle{H\otimes\left(A\otimes(H\otimes B)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{n}_{A,H\otimes B}^{\Delta}}
\textstyle{(H\otimes A)\otimes\left(H\otimes(H\otimes B)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(1_{H}\otimes 1_{A})\otimes(\mathsf{S}\otimes(1_{H}\otimes 1_{B}))}
\textstyle{(H\otimes A)\otimes\left(H\otimes(H\otimes B)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(1_{H}\otimes 1_{A})\otimes\mu^{\mathsf{m}}_{B}}
\textstyle{(H\otimes A)\otimes(H\otimes B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{H,A,H\otimes B}}
\textstyle{H\otimes\left(A\otimes(H\otimes B)\right)}

and the inverse of the right fusion operator is defined similarly. Therefore if
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
is closed, then so is
\left(\mathsf{MOD}(H,\nabla,\mathsf{u}),\otimes^{\mathsf{n}^{\Delta}},(K,\mathsf{n}^{\mathsf{e}}_{K})\right)
. Conversly if
(H\otimes-,\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
is a symmetric Hopf monad on
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
, then
(A,\mathsf{m}^{\mu},\mathsf{u}^{\eta},\Delta^{\mathsf{n}},\mathsf{e}^{n_{K}},\mathsf{S}^{\mathsf{h}})
is a cocommutative Hopf monoid where the cocommutative bimonoid structure is defined as in Example
3.2
while the antipode
\mathsf{S}^{\mathsf{h}}:H\to H
is defined as the following composite:

\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{-1}_{H}}
\textstyle{H\otimes K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{-1}_{H\otimes K}}
\textstyle{(H\otimes K)\otimes K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(1_{H}\otimes 1_{K})\otimes\eta^{\mathsf{u}}_{K}}
\textstyle{(H\otimes K)\otimes(H\otimes K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{h}^{\ell^{-1}}_{K,K}}
\textstyle{H\otimes\left(K\otimes(H\otimes K)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{-1}_{H,K,H\otimes K}}
\textstyle{(H\otimes K)\otimes(H\otimes K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{n}^{\mathsf{e}}_{K}\otimes\rho_{H}}
\textstyle{K\otimes H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ell_{H}}
\textstyle{H}

For more details on this example and a string diagram representation, see
[
BLV11
, Example 2.10]
.

3.4.
Comonads and their Coalgebras

The dual notion of a monad is that a comonad. Comonads are key structures in categorical semantics of the exponential fragment of linear logic. Throughout this paper, we will encounter various different kinds of comonads with added structure such as symmetric monoidal comonads (Definition
3.5
), coalgebra modalities (Definition
4.1
), and monoidal coalgebra modalities (Definition
4.2
).

{defi}

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a category.

(1)

A
comonad

[
ML13
]
on a category
\mathbb{X}
is a triple
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
consisting of a functor
{\oc:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}}
and two natural transformations
\delta_{A}:\oc(A)\to\oc\oc(A)
and
\varepsilon_{A}:\oc(A)\to A
such that the following diagrams commute:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 15.31537pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-12.0278pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern (26)

(3)

For each object
A
, the
cofree
\oc
-coalgebra
over
A
is the
\oc
-coalgebra
(\oc(A),\delta_{A})
.

(27)

(5)

The category of
\oc
-coalgebras and
\oc
-coalgebra morphisms is denoted
\mathbb{X}^{\oc}
and is also known as the
Eilenberg-Moore category of coalgebras
of the comonad
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
. There is a forgetful functor
\mathsf{U}^{\oc}:\mathbb{X}^{\oc}\to\mathbb{X}
, which is defined on objects as
\mathsf{U}^{\oc}(A,\omega)=A
and on maps as
\mathsf{U}^{\oc}(f)=f
.

As comonads are dual to monads, comonoids induce examples of comonads in the dual sense of Example
3.1
. Though these are not usually the examples of comonads of interest in linear logic. We discuss examples of comonads of interest in linear logic and to this paper in Example
4.2
.

3.5.
Symmetric Monoidal Comonads

{defi}

Let
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
be a symmetric monoidal category. A
symmetric monoidal endofunctor
on
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
is a triple
(\oc,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
consisting of an endofunctor
\oc:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
, a natural transformation
\mathsf{m}_{A,B}:\oc(A)\otimes\oc(B)\to\oc(A\otimes B)
, and a map
{\mathsf{m}_{K}:K\to\oc(K)}
such that the following diagrams commute:

(28)

A
symmetric monoidal comonad
on
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
is a quintuple
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
consisting of a comonad
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
and a symmetric monoidal endofunctor
(\oc,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
such that the following diagrams commute:

(29)

As symmetric monoidal comonads are the dual notion of symmetric comonoidal monads, their Eilenberg-Moore categories are again symmetric monoidal categories. Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
be a symmetric monoidal comonad on a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
. Then (abusing notation slightly)
\left(\mathbb{X}^{\oc},\otimes^{\mathsf{m}},(K,\mathsf{m}_{K}),\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\ell},\tilde{\rho},\tilde{\sigma}\right)
is a symmetric monoidal category where in particular the tensor product
\otimes^{\mathsf{m}}
of a pair of
\oc
-coalgebras
(A,\omega)
and
(B,\omega^{\prime})
, is defined as the pair
(A,\omega)\otimes^{\mathsf{m}}(B,\omega^{\prime}):=(A\otimes B,\omega\otimes^{\mathsf{n}}\omega^{\prime})
where
\omega\otimes^{\mathsf{m}}\omega^{\prime}
is defined as follows:

\begin{gathered}\omega\otimes^{\mathsf{m}}\omega^{\prime}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 16.90448pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-16.90448pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\otimes B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.38927pt\raise 6.31447pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.48112pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\omega\otimes\omega^{\prime}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 76.90448pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 76.90448pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A)\otimes\oc(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 149.48755pt\raise 5.54443pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.56667pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{m}_{A,B}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 191.82462pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 191.82462pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A\otimes B)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(30)

while the tensor product of
\oc
-coalgebra morphisms
f
and
g
is defined as
f\otimes^{\mathsf{m}}g:=f\otimes g
. The diagrams of (
29
) state that
\mathsf{m}
is a
\oc
-coalgebra morphism and that
(K,\mathsf{m}_{K})
is a
\oc
-coalgebra. The diagrams of (
28
) are the required coherences to make
\alpha
,
\ell
,
\rho
, and
\sigma
all
\oc
-coalgebra morphisms.

4.
Coalgebra Modalities and Linear Categories

In this section, we review the categorical semantics of the exponential modality in the multiplicative and exponential fragment of linear logic (
\mathsf{MELL}
). In particular, we review the notions of coalgebra modalities, monoidal coalgebra modalities, and linear categories. We also discuss the particular case when the monoidal coalgebra modality is induced by cofree cocommutative comonoids, and linear categories of this form are better known as Lafont categories. For more details and an in-depth introduction on the categorical semantics of
\mathsf{MELL}
we refer the reader to
[
Bie95
,
HS03
,
Mel03
,
Mel09
]
.

4.1.
Coalgebra Modalities

Coalgebra modalities were defined by Blute, Cockett, and Seely when they introduced differential categories
[
BCS06
]
and are a strictly weaker notion of monoidal coalgebra modalities. While monoidal coalgebra modalities are much more popular as they give categorical models of the exponential fragment of linear logic, coalgebra modalities have sufficient structure to axiomatize differentiation. Therefore, we believe it of interest to discuss coalgebra modalities in order to also discuss lifting differential category structure (see Section
7
). Interesting examples of coalgebra modalities which are not monoidal can be found in
[
BCLS19
]
.

{defi}

A
coalgebra modality

[
BCS06
]
on a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
is a quintuple
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
consisting of a comonad
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
, a natural transformation
{\Delta_{A}:\oc(A)\to\oc(A)\otimes\oc(A)}
, and a natural transformation
\mathsf{e}_{A}:\oc(A)\to K
such that for each object
A
, the triple
(\oc(A),\Delta_{A},\mathsf{e}_{A})
is a cocommutative comonoid and
\delta_{A}:(\oc(A),\Delta_{A},\mathsf{e}_{A})\to(\oc\oc(A),\Delta_{\oc(A)},\mathsf{e}_{\oc(A)})
is a comonoid morphism.

Note that naturality of
\Delta
and
\mathsf{e}
is equivalent to asking that for every map
f:A\to B
,
\oc(f):(\oc(A),\Delta_{A},\mathsf{e}_{A})\to(\oc(B),\Delta_{B},\mathsf{e}_{B})
is a comonoid morphism. Therefore it immediately follows that every comonad on a Cartesian monoidal category is a coalgebra modality.

Lemma 1
.

Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
be a comonad on a Cartesian monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
. Then
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,{\hat{\Delta}},\mathsf{e})
is a coalgebra modality on
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
where
(\oc(A),{\hat{\Delta}}_{A},\mathsf{e}_{A})
is the unique cocommutative comonoid structure on
\oc(A)
from Lemma
2.2
.

Every
\oc
-coalgebra of a coalgebra modality comes equipped with a cocommutative comonoid structure
[
Mel09
,
Sch04
]
. Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
be a coalgebra modality on a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
and
(A,\omega)
an
\oc
-coalgebra of
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
. Then the triple
(A,\Delta^{\omega},\mathsf{e}^{\omega})
is a cocommutative comonoid where the comultiplication and counit are defined as follows:

\begin{gathered}\Delta^{\omega}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 66.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 66.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 111.68056pt\raise 5.89445pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.8889pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\Delta_{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 150.8056pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern \mathsf{e}^{\omega}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.23112pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\omega}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 66.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 113.40973pt\raise 5.05833pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.05278pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{e}_{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 150.8056pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 150.8056pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{K}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(31)

Notice that since
\delta_{A}
is a comonoid morphism, when applying this construction to a cofree
\oc
-coalgebra
(\oc(A),\delta_{A})
we re-obtain
\Delta_{A}
and
\mathsf{e}_{A}
, that is,
\Delta_{A}^{\delta_{A}}=\Delta_{A}
and
\mathsf{e}_{A}^{\delta_{A}}=\mathsf{e}_{A}
. Furthermore, by naturality of
\Delta_{A}
and
\mathsf{e}_{A}
, every
\oc
-coalgebra morphism becomes a comonoid morphism on the induced comonoid structures. In particular this implies that for every
\oc
-coalgebra
(A,\omega)
, we have that
\omega
is a comonoid morphism, that is, the following diagrams commute:

4.2.
Monoidal Coalgebra Modalities and Linear Categories

{defi}

A
monoidal coalgebra modality

[
BCS15
]
(also called a
linear exponential modality

[
Sch04
]
) on a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
is a septuple
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
such that
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
is a symmetric monoidal comonad and
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
is a coalgebra modality, and such that
\Delta
and
\mathsf{e}
are monoidal transformations, that is, the following diagrams commute:
