(34)

A
linear category

[
Bie95
]
is a symmetric monoidal closed category with a monoidal coalgebra modality.

There are many equivalent alternative descriptions of a monoidal coalgebra modality
[
Sch04
]
. Of particular interest to this paper is that a monoidal coalgebra modality can be equivalently be described as a symmetric monoidal comonad whose Eilenberg-Moore category is a Cartesian monoidal category (Definition
2.1
). So if
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
is a monoidal coalgebra modality on a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
, then
\left(\mathbb{X}^{\oc},\otimes^{\mathsf{m}},(K,\mathsf{m}_{K})\right)
(as defined in Section
3.5
) is a Cartesian monoidal category. Furthermore, the canonical cocommutative comonoid structure from Lemma
2.2
on a
\oc
-coalgebra
(A,\omega)
is precisely given by
\Delta^{\omega}
and
\mathsf{e}^{\omega}
as defined in (
31
), that is,
((A,\omega),\Delta^{\omega},\mathsf{e}^{\omega})
is a cocommutative comonoid in
\left(\mathbb{X}^{\oc},\otimes^{\mathsf{m}},(K,\mathsf{m}_{K})\right)
. As linear categories are categorical models of
\mathsf{MELL}

[
Bie95
,
Mel03
,
Mel09
]
, there is no shortage of examples of linear categories throughout the literature. We provide some examples of linear categories in Example
4.2
, while Hyland and Schalk provide a very nice list of various kinds examples in
[
HS03
, Section 2.4]
.

An important source of examples of monoidal coalgebra modalities are those for which
\oc(A)
is also the cofree cocommutative comonoid over
A
(as shown by Lafont in his Ph.D. thesis
[
Laf88
]
). Monoidal coalgebra modalities with this extra couniversal property are known as
free exponential modalities

[
MTT17
]
and models of linear logic with free exponential modalities are known as
Lafont categories

[
Mel09
]
. While free exponential modalities have been around since the beginning with Girard’s free exponential modality for coherence spaces
[
Gir87
]
, new free exponential modalities are still being constructed and studied
[
CEPT17
,
LMMP13
,
Sla17
]
, which shows the importance of these kinds models. In fact, Lafont categories are by far the most common examples of linear categories.

{defi}

A monoidal coalgebra modality
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
is said to be a
free exponential modality

[
MTT17
,
Sla17
]
if for each object
A
,
\oc(A)
is a
cofree cocommutative comonoid
over
A
, that is, if
(C,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
is a cocommutative comonoid, then for every map
f:C\to A
there exists a unique comonoid morphism
\hat{f}:(C,\Delta,\mathsf{e})\to(\oc(A),\Delta,\mathsf{e})
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists\oc~\hat{f}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{A}}
\textstyle{A}

A
Lafont category

[
Mel03
]
is a linear category whose monoidal coalgebra modality is a free exponential modality.

Observe that every Cartesian monoidal category trivially admits a free exponential modality.

Lemma 2
.

Let
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
be a Cartesian monoidal category and let
1_{\mathbb{X}}:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
be the identity functor. Then
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{t},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
is a free exponential modality where
\delta_{A}=\varepsilon_{A}=1_{A}
,
\mathsf{m}_{A,B}=1_{A}\otimes 1_{B}
,
\mathsf{m}_{K}=1_{K}
, and
(A,\Delta_{A},\mathsf{t}_{A})
is the unique cocommutative comonoid structure on
A
from Lemma
2.2
. Therefore every Cartesian closed category is a Lafont category.

The Eilenberg-Moore category of a free exponential modality is equivalent to the category of cocommutative comonoids of the base symmetric monoidal category
[
Mel03
,
Mel09
]
.

{exas}

We conclude this section by briefly giving some examples of Lafont categories (and therefore examples of linear categories). In particular, we give examples of free exponential modalities on the symmetric monoidal closed categories of Example
2.1
. Many other examples of free exponential modalities can be found in
[
HS03
]
, while detailed constructions of free exponential modalities can be found in
[
MTT17
,
Sla17
]
.

(1)

Since
(\mathsf{SET},\times,\{\ast\})
is a Cartesian closed category, it follows from Lemma
1
that every comonad on
\mathsf{SET}
is a coalgebra modality and also by Lemma
2
that
(\mathsf{SET},\times,\{\ast\})
is trivially a Lafont category where the free exponential modality is given by the identity functor.

(2)

(\mathsf{REL},\times,\{\ast\})
is a Lafont category with free exponential modality
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{\{\ast\}})
where:

(i)
The endofunctor
\oc:\mathsf{REL}\to\mathsf{REL}
maps a set
X
to the set of all finite bags (also known as multisets) of
X
:

\oc(X)=\{\llbracket x_{1},...,x_{n}\rrbracket|~x_{i}\in X\}

while for relation
R:X\to Y
,
\oc(R):\oc(X)\to\oc(Y)
is the relation which relates a bag of
X
to a bag of
Y
of the same size and such that the elements of the bags are related:

\oc(R)=\{(\llbracket x_{1},...,x_{n}\rrbracket,\llbracket y_{1},...,y_{n}\rrbracket)|~(x_{i},y_{i})\in R\}\subseteq\oc(X)\times\oc(Y)

(ii)

\varepsilon_{X}:\oc(X)\to X
is the relation which relates one element bags to their element:

\varepsilon_{X}=\{(\llbracket x\rrbracket,x)|~x\in X\}\subseteq\oc(X)\times X

(iii)

\delta_{X}:\oc(X)\to\oc(\oc(X))
is the relation which relates a bag to the bag of all possible bag splittings of the original bag:

\displaystyle\delta_{X}=\{(B,\llbracket B_{1},...,B_{n}\rrbracket)|~B,B_{i}\in\oc(X),~B_{1}\sqcup...\sqcup B_{n}=B\}\subseteq\oc(X)\times\oc\oc(X)

(iv)

\Delta_{X}:\oc(X)\to\oc(X)\times\oc(X)
is the relation which relates a bag to the pair of all possible two bag splittings of the original bag:

\Delta_{X}=\{(B,(B_{1},B_{2}))|~B,B_{i}\in\oc(X),~B_{1}\sqcup B_{2}=B\}\subseteq\oc(X)\times\left(\oc(X)\times\oc(X)\right)

(v)

\mathsf{e}_{X}:\oc(X)\to\{\ast\}
is the relation which relates the empty bag to the single element:

\mathsf{e}_{X}=\{(\emptyset,\ast)\}\subseteq\oc(X)\times\{\ast\}

(vi)

\mathsf{m}_{X,Y}:\oc(X)\times\oc(Y)\to\oc(X\times Y)
is the relation which relates bags of
X
and bags of
Y
of the same size to their Cartesian product bag:

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}_{X,Y}=

\displaystyle\{(\llbracket x_{1},...,x_{n}\rrbracket,\llbracket y_{1},...,y_{n}\rrbracket,\llbracket(x_{i},y_{j})|~0\leq i,j\leq n\rrbracket)|~x_{i}\in X,y_{j}\in Y\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\left(\oc(X)\times\oc(Y)\right)\times\oc(X\times Y)

(vii)

\mathsf{m}_{\{\ast\}}:\{\ast\}\to\oc(\{\ast\})
is the relation which relates
\ast
to every element of
\oc(\{\ast\})
:

\mathsf{m}_{\{\ast\}}:=\{(\ast,B)|~B\in\oc(\{\ast\})\}\subseteq\{\ast\}\times\oc(\{\ast\})

For more details on the free exponential modality of
(\mathsf{REL},\times,\{\ast\})
see
[
BCS06
,
HS03
]
.

(3)

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a field. Then
(\mathsf{VEC}_{\mathbb{K}},\otimes,\mathbb{K})
is a Lafont category where full details of the free exponential modality and discussions on cofree cocommutative
\mathbb{K}
-coalgebras can be found in
[
HS03
,
Mur15
,
Swe69
]
. In
[
Mur15
]
, Murfet provides a nice expression of cofree cocommutative
\mathbb{K}
-coalgebras when
\mathbb{K}
is algebraically closed and of characteristic
0
, say for example the complex numbers
\mathbb{C}
. As a simple example in that case, the cofree cocommutative
\mathbb{K}
-coalgebra over the field
\mathbb{K}
itself is isomorphic as
\mathbb{K}
-coalgebra to:

\oc(\mathbb{K})\cong\bigoplus\limits_{k\in\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{K}[x]

where
\mathbb{K}[x]
is the polynomial ring in one variable.

5.
Symmetric Monoidal Mixed Distributive Laws

Distributive laws between monads, which are natural transformations satisfying certain coherences with the monad structures, were introduced by Beck
[
Bec69
]
in order to both compose monads and lift one monad to the other’s Eilenberg-Moore category. By lifting we mean that the forgetful functor from the Eilenberg-Moore category to base category preserves the monad strictly. In fact, there is a bijective correspondence between distributive laws between monads and lifting of monads. From a higher category theory perspective, a distributive law is a monad on the
2
-category of monads of a
2
-category
[
Str72
]
. There are also several other notions of distributive laws involving monads and bijective correspondence with certain liftings
[
Wis08
]
. Of interest to this paper are mixed distributive laws of monads over comonads
[
Bec69
,
HHM07
]
and in particular mixed distributive laws of symmetric comonoidal monads over symmetric monoidal comonads. This latter kind of mixed distributive law will be in used in Section
6
to lift coalgebra modalities and linear category structure, which is the main goal of this paper. For a more detailed introduction on distributive laws and liftings see
[
Wis08
]
.

5.1.
Mixed Distributive Laws

{defi}

Let
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta)
be a monad and
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
a comonad on the same category
\mathbb{X}
. A
mixed distributive law

[
HHM07
,
Wis08
]
of
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta)
over
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
is a natural transformation
{\lambda_{A}:\mathsf{T}\oc(A)\to\oc\mathsf{T}(A)}
such that the following diagrams commute:

77.25006pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 145.06953pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc\oc\mathsf{T}(A)}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 239.13907pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 306.95856pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{T}(A)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(36)

As with distributive laws between monads, mixed distributive laws allow one to lift comonads to Eilenberg-Moore categories of monads and also to lift monads to Eilenberg-Moore categories of comonads, such that the respective forgetful functors preserve the monads or comonads strictly. In fact, mixed distributive laws are in bijective correspondence with these liftings.

{thmC}

[
[
VO71
, Theorem IV.1]
] Let
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta)
be a monad and
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
be a comonad on the same category
\mathbb{X}
. Then the following are in bijective correspondence:

(1)

Mixed distributive laws of
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta)
over
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
;

(2)

Liftings of the comonad
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
to
\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}}
, that is, a comonad
(\tilde{\oc},\tilde{\delta},\tilde{\varepsilon})
on
\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}}
such that the forgetful functor preserves the comonad strictly, that is, the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\oc}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\oc}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}}

and for each
\mathsf{T}
-algebra
(A,\nu)
,
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}(\tilde{\delta}_{(A,\nu)})=\delta_{A}
and
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{(A,\nu)})=\varepsilon_{A}
.

(3)

Liftings of the monad
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta)
to
\mathbb{X}^{\oc}
, that is, a monad
(\tilde{\mathsf{T}},\tilde{\mu_{A}},\tilde{\eta_{A}})
on
\mathbb{X}^{\oc}
such that the forgetful functor
\mathsf{U}^{\oc}
preserves the comonad strictly, that is, the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}^{\oc}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{U}^{\oc}}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\mathsf{T}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}^{\oc}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{U}^{\oc}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{T}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}}

and for each
\oc
-coalgebra
(A,\omega)
,
\mathsf{U}^{\oc}(\tilde{\mu}_{(A,\omega)})=\mu_{A}
and
\mathsf{U}^{\oc}(\tilde{\eta}_{(A,\omega)})=\eta_{A}
.

We briefly review how to construct mixed distributive laws from liftings and vice-versa (for more details see
[
Wis08
]
). Let
\lambda_{A}
be a mixed distributive law of a monad
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta)
over a comonad
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
over the same category
\mathbb{X}
. For a
\mathsf{T}
-algebra
(A,\nu)
, define the map
\nu^{\sharp}:\mathsf{T}\oc(A)\to\oc(A)
as follows:

\begin{gathered}\nu^{\sharp}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 15.43059pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-15.43059pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{T}\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 37.21873pt\raise 5.93333pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.92778pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\lambda_{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 75.43059pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 75.43059pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc\mathsf{T}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 127.03017pt\raise 6.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\oc(\nu)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 166.29176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 166.29176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(A)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(37)

Then the pair
(\oc(A),\nu^{\sharp})
is a
\mathsf{T}
-algebra. Dually, if
(A,\omega)
is a
\oc
-coalgebra, then define the map
\omega^{\flat}:\mathsf{T}A\to\oc\mathsf{T}(A)
as follows:

\begin{gathered}\omega^{\flat}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 10.15279pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-10.15279pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{T}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.12694pt\raise 6.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{T}(\omega)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 70.15279pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 70.15279pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{T}\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 122.80211pt\raise 5.93333pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.92778pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\lambda_{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 161.01396pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 161.01396pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc\mathsf{T}A}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(38)

Then the pair
(\mathsf{T}(A),\omega^{\flat})
is a
\oc
-coalgebra. The induced comonad
(\tilde{\oc},\tilde{\delta},\tilde{\varepsilon})
on
\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}}
and the induced comonad
(\tilde{\mathsf{T}},\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\eta})
on
\mathbb{X}^{\oc}
are defined in the obvious way.

Conversly, to obtain a mixed distributive law, we can either start with a lifting of a monad to the Eilenberg-Moore category of a comonad, or a lifting of a comonad to the Eilenberg-Moore category of a monad. We review explicitly the latter of these options as this is the construction we will use in many of the upcoming proofs of this paper. Let
(\tilde{\oc},\tilde{\delta},\tilde{\varepsilon})
be a lifting of
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
to
\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}}
. This implies that for each free
\mathsf{T}
-algebra
(\mathsf{T}(A),\mu_{A})
we have that
\tilde{\oc}(\mathsf{T}(A),\mu_{A})=(\oc\mathsf{T}(A),\mu_{A}^{\sharp})
for some map
\mu_{A}^{\sharp}:\mathsf{T}\oc\mathsf{T}(A)\to\oc\mathsf{T}(A)
. Define the natural transformation
\lambda_{A}:\mathsf{T}\oc(A)\to\oc\mathsf{T}(A)
as follows:

\begin{gathered}\lambda_{A}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 15.43059pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-15.43059pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{T}\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.89127pt\raise 6.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{T}\oc(\eta_{A})}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 75.43059pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 75.43059pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{T}\oc\mathsf{T}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 134.82587pt\raise 6.92557pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.56447pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mu_{A}^{\sharp}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 173.09734pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 173.09734pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc\mathsf{T}(A)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(39)

Then
\lambda_{A}
is a mixed distributive law of
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta)
over
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
.

5.2.
Symmetric Monoidal Mixed Distributive Laws

We now extend the definition of a mixed distributive law to a special mixed distributive law of symmetric comonoidal monads over symmetric monoidal comonads such that the liftings are again symmetric (co)monoidal. At this point, the author would like to thank the reviewers for their suggestion of expanding out this part of the story, which was not done in the extended abstract version of this paper
[
Lem18
]
.

{defi}

Let
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
be a symmetric comonoidal monad and
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
a symmetric monoidal comonad on the same symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
. A
symmetric monoidal mixed distributive law
of
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
over
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
is a mixed distributive law
\lambda
of
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta)
over
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
such that the following diagrams commute:

76.28474pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 168.85423pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\oc(K)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(40)

As we will see in the Proposition
3
, the coherences of a symmetric monoidal mixed distributive law are precisely the requirements needed in order to lift symmetric monoidal comonads to the Eilenberg-Moore categories of symmetric comonoidal monads, and vice-versa. In particular, the left diagram of (
40
) implies that
\mathsf{m}
is a
\mathsf{T}
-algebra morphism and similarly that
\mathsf{n}
is a
\oc
-coalgebra morphism. While the right diagram of (
40
) implies that
(K,\mathsf{m}_{K})
is a
\mathsf{T}
-algebra and that
(K,\mathsf{n}_{K})
is a
\oc
-coalgebra.

Proposition 3
.

Let
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
be a symmetric comonoidal monad and
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
a symmetric monoidal comonad on the same symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
. Then the following are in bijective correspondence:

(1)

Symmetric monoidal mixed distributive laws of
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
over
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
;

(2)

Liftings of the symmetric monoidal comonad
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
to
(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}},\otimes^{\mathsf{n}},(K,\mathsf{n}_{K}))
, that is, a symmetric monoidal comonad
(\tilde{\oc},\tilde{\delta},\tilde{\varepsilon},\tilde{\mathsf{m}},\tilde{\mathsf{m}}_{(K,\mathsf{n}_{K})})
on
(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}},\otimes^{\mathsf{n}},(K,\mathsf{n}_{K}))
which is a lifting of the underlying comonad to
\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}}
(in the sense of Theorem
5.1
) such that for each pair of
\mathsf{T}
-algebras
(A,\nu)
and
(B,\nu^{\prime})
,
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}(\tilde{\mathsf{m}}_{(A,\nu),(B,\nu^{\prime})})=\mathsf{m}_{A,B}
, and also that
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}(\tilde{\mathsf{m}}_{(K,\mathsf{n}_{K})})=\mathsf{m}_{K}
.

(3)

Liftings of the symmetric comonoidal monad
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
to
(\mathbb{X}^{\oc},\otimes^{\mathsf{m}},(K,\mathsf{m}_{K}))
, that is, a symmetric comonoidal monad
(\tilde{\mathsf{T}},\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\mathsf{n}},\tilde{\mathsf{n}}_{(K,\mathsf{m}_{K})})
on
(\mathbb{X}^{\oc},\otimes^{\mathsf{m}},(K,\mathsf{m}_{K}))
which is a lifting of the underlying monad to
\mathbb{X}^{\oc}
(in the sense of Theorem
5.1
) such that for each pair of
\oc
-algebras
(A,\omega)
and
(B,\omega^{\prime})
,
\mathsf{U}^{\oc}(\tilde{\mathsf{n}}_{(A,\omega),(B,\omega^{\prime})})=\mathsf{n}_{A,B}
, and also that
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}(\tilde{\mathsf{n}}_{(K,\mathsf{m}_{K})})=\mathsf{n}_{K}
.

Proof 5.1
.

The bijective correspondence will follow from Theorem
5.1
. It remains to show that the induced lifting of the comonad (resp. monad) from the mixed distributive law is also a lifting of the symmetric monoidal comonad (resp. symmetric comonoidal monad), and similarly that the induced mixed distributive law from the liftings also satisfies (
40
).

Let
\lambda
be a symmetric mixed distributive law of
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
over
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
. Consider first the induced lifting of
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
from Theorem
5.1
. To prove that we have a lifting of the symmetric monoidal comonad, it suffices to show that
\mathsf{m}
and
\mathsf{m}_{K}
are
\mathsf{T}
-algebra morphisms. The right diagram of (
40
) is precisely the statement that
\mathsf{m}_{K}
is a
\mathsf{T}
-algebra morphism. Then if
(A,\nu)
and
(B,\nu^{\prime})
are
\mathsf{T}
-algebras, commutativity of the following diagrams show that
\mathsf{m}_{A,B}
is a
\mathsf{T}
-algebra morphism:

\scriptstyle{(\ref{mixedsymco})}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{T}(\mathsf{m}_{A,B})}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{n}_{A,B}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{T}\oc(A)\otimes\mathsf{T}\oc(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{A}\otimes\lambda_{B}}
\textstyle{\oc\mathsf{T}(A)\otimes\oc\mathsf{T}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
Nat. of
\mathsf{m}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{T}(A),\mathsf{T}(B)}}

\textstyle{\oc(A)\otimes\oc(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\oc(A\otimes B)}

\oc

\textstyle{\oc(K)}

One could have also started with the lifting of the symmetric comonoidal monad to obtain the desired symmetric monoidal mixed distributive law.

As the main goal of this paper is to lift linear category structure to categories of modules over monoids, we now study symmetric monoidal mixed distributive laws in the case when the symmetric comonoidal monad is induced by a cocommutative bimonoid (Example
3.2
).

Proposition 4
.

Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
be a symmetric monoidal comonad on a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
. Then the following are in bijective correspondence:

(1)

Cocommutative bimonoids in
(\mathbb{X}^{\oc},\otimes^{\mathsf{m}},(K,\mathsf{m}_{K}))
;

(2)

Cocommutative bimonoids
(A,\nabla,\mathsf{u},\Delta,\mathsf{e})
in
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
equipped with a natural transformation
\lambda_{X}:A\otimes\oc(X)\to\oc(A\otimes X)
such that
\lambda
is a symmetric monoidal mixed distributive law of
(A\otimes-,\mu^{\nabla},\eta^{\mathsf{u}},\mathsf{n}^{\Delta},\mathsf{n}^{\mathsf{e}}_{K})
(as defined in Example
3.2
) over
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
and the following diagram commutes:

(43)

Proof 5.2
.

We first show that we can construct one from the other.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
: Let
((A,\omega),\nabla,\mathsf{u},\Delta,\mathsf{e})
be a cocommutative bimonoid in
(\mathbb{X}^{\oc},\otimes^{\mathsf{m}},(K,\mathsf{m}_{K}))
. Explicitly this means that
(A,\omega)
is a
\oc
-coalgebra, that
(A,\nabla,\mathsf{u},\Delta,\mathsf{e})
is a cocommutative bimonoid in
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,\mathbb{K})
, and that
\nabla
,
\mathsf{u}
,
\Delta
, and
\mathsf{e}
are all
\oc
-coalgebra morphisms, that is, the following diagrams commute:

6.
Mixed Distributive Laws for Coalgebra Modalities

In this section, we lift both coalgebra modalities and monoidal coalgebra modalities to Eilenberg-Moore categories of symmetric comonoidal monads, the latter of which requires symmetric monoidal mixed distributive laws. We also introduce the notions of exponential lifting monads and
\mathsf{MELL}
lifting monads, where in particular the Eilenberg-Moore category of a
\mathsf{MELL}
lifting monad is a linear category – which is the main result of this paper.

6.1.
Lifting Coalgebra Modalities

In order to lift coalgebra modalities to Eilenberg-Moore categories of symmetric comonoidal monads, one requires that
\Delta
and
\mathsf{e}
be
\mathsf{T}
-algebra morphism. This can equivalently be expressed as needing a mixed distributive law which is also a comonoid morphism since symmetric comonoidal endofunctors preserve cocommutative comonoids
[
Moe02
]
. Indeed, let
(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
be a symmetric comonoidal endofunctor on a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
. If
(A,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
is a cocommutative comonoid in
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
then so is the triple
(\mathsf{T}(A),\Delta^{\mathsf{T}},\mathsf{e}^{\mathsf{T}})
where the comultiplication and counit are defined as follows:

\begin{gathered}\Delta^{\mathsf{T}}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 14.04169pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-14.04169pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{T}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.24998pt\raise 6.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{T}(\Delta)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 74.04169pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 74.04169pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{T}(A\otimes A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 140.6013pt\raise 5.54443pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.56667pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{n}_{A,A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 181.84723pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 181.84723pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{T}(A)\otimes\mathsf{T}(A)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\\

(49)

Note that if
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
is a Cartesian monoidal category, then
(\mathsf{T}(A),\Delta^{\mathsf{T}},\mathsf{e}^{\mathsf{T}})
must be equal to the unique cocommutative comonoid structure on
\mathsf{T}(A)
from Lemma
2.2
.

{defi}

Let
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
be a symmetric comonoidal monad and
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
be a coalgebra modality on the same symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
. A
coalgebra mixed distributive law
of
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
over
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
is a mixed distributive law
\lambda
of
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta)
over
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
such that
\lambda_{A}:(\mathsf{T}\oc(A),\Delta_{A}^{\mathsf{T}},\mathsf{e}_{A}^{\mathsf{T}})\to(\oc\mathsf{T}(A),\Delta_{\mathsf{T}(A)},\mathsf{e}_{\mathsf{T}(A)})
is a comonoid morphism, that is, the following diagrams commute:

Proposition 7
.

Let
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
be a symmetric comonoidal monad and
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
be a coalgebra modality on the same symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
. Then the following are in bijective correspondence:

(1)

Coalgebra mixed distributive laws of
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
over
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
;

(2)

Liftings of
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
to
(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}},\otimes^{\mathsf{n}},(K,\mathsf{n}_{K}))
, that is, a coalgebra modality
(\tilde{\oc},\tilde{\delta},\tilde{\varepsilon},\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\mathsf{e}})
on
(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}},\otimes^{\mathsf{n}},(K,\mathsf{n}_{K}))
which is a lifting of the underlying comonad
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
to
\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}}
(in the sense of Theorem
5.1
) such that for every
\oc
-algebras
(A,\omega)
,
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}(\tilde{\Delta}_{(A,\omega)})=\Delta_{A}
and
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}(\tilde{\mathsf{e}}_{(A,\omega)})=\mathsf{e}_{A}
.

Proof 6.2
.

3
. The bijective correspondence will follow from Theorem

, and so it remains to show that we obtain one from the other.

(52)

where recall
\mu_{A}^{\sharp}
is the
\mathsf{T}
-algebra structure of
\tilde{\oc}(\mathsf{T}(A),\mu_{A})=(\oc\mathsf{T}(A),\mu_{A}^{\sharp})
. Consider now the induced mixed distributive law
\lambda
of
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta)
over
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon)
as defined in (

). Then that
\lambda
is a comonoid morphism, and therefore also a coalgebra mixed distributive law, follows from commutativity of the following diagrams:

6.2.
Lifting Monoidal Coalgebra Modalities

We first show that for monoidal coalgebra modalities, every symmetric monoidal mixed distributive law is in fact also a coalgebra mixed distributive law.

Lemma 8
.

Let
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
be a symmetric comonoidal monad and
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
be a monoidal coalgebra modality on the same symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
, and let
\lambda
be a symmetric monoidal mixed distributive law of
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
over
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
. Then
\lambda
is a coalgebra mixed distributive law of
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
over
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
.

Proof 6.3
.

Let
\lambda
be a symmetric monoidal mixed distributive law of
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
over

(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
. By Proposition
3
, this induces a symmetric comonoidal monad

(\tilde{\mathsf{T}},\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\mathsf{n}},\tilde{\mathsf{n}}_{(K,\mathsf{m}_{K})})
on the Eilenberg-Moore category
(\mathbb{X}^{\oc},\otimes^{\mathsf{m}},(K,\mathsf{m}_{K}))
, which in particular maps cocommutative comonoids to cocommutative comonoids. Since for every
\oc
-coalgebra
(A,\omega)
,
((A,\omega),\Delta^{\omega},\mathsf{e}^{\omega})
is a cocommutative comonoid in
(\mathbb{X}^{\oc},\otimes^{\mathsf{m}},(K,\mathsf{m}_{K}))
, then so is
\left((\mathsf{T}(A),\omega^{\flat}),(\Delta^{\omega})^{\mathsf{T}},(\mathsf{e}^{\omega})^{\mathsf{T}}\right)
, where
\omega^{\flat}
is defined as in (
38
), and
(\Delta^{\omega})^{\mathsf{T}}
and
(\mathsf{e}^{\omega})^{\mathsf{T}}
are defined as in (
49
). However
(\mathbb{X}^{\oc},\otimes^{\mathsf{m}},(K,\mathsf{m}_{K}))
is a Cartesian monoidal category and therefore by Lemma
2.2
we have:

(\Delta^{\omega})^{\mathsf{T}}=\Delta^{\omega^{\flat}}\quad\quad(\mathsf{e}^{\omega})^{\mathsf{T}}=\mathsf{e}^{\omega^{\flat}}

Applying (
38
) to a cofree
\oc
-coalgebra
(\oc(A),\delta_{A})
, we obtain the
\oc
-coalgebra
(\mathsf{T}\oc(A),\delta_{A}^{\flat})
and that
\lambda_{A}:(\mathsf{T}\oc(A),\delta_{A}^{\flat})\to(\oc\mathsf{T}(A),\delta_{\mathsf{T}(A)})
is a
\oc
-coalgebra morphism. Since
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
is a coalgebra modality we also have that
\lambda_{A}:(\mathsf{T}\oc(A),\Delta^{\delta_{A}^{\flat}},\mathsf{e}^{\delta_{A}^{\flat}})\to(\oc\mathsf{T}(A),\Delta_{\mathsf{T}(A)},\mathsf{e}_{\mathsf{T}(A)})
is a comonoid morphism (since recall that
\Delta^{\delta_{A}}=\Delta_{A}
and
\mathsf{e}^{\delta_{A}}=\mathsf{e}_{A}
). However applying the above identity to to a cofree
\oc
-coalgebra
(\oc(A),\delta_{A})
we get that
\Delta_{A}^{\mathsf{T}}=\Delta^{\delta_{A}^{\flat}}
and that
\mathsf{e}_{A}^{\mathsf{T}}=\mathsf{e}^{\delta_{A}^{\flat}}
. Therefore we conclude that
\lambda_{A}:(\mathsf{T}\oc(A),\Delta_{A}^{\mathsf{T}},\mathsf{e}_{A}^{\mathsf{T}})\to(\oc\mathsf{T}(A),\Delta_{\mathsf{T}(A)},\mathsf{e}_{\mathsf{T}(A)})
is a comonoid morphism and that
\lambda
is a coalgebra mixed distributive law.

Gathering all of our results together, we can show that symmetric monoidal mixed distributive laws are precisely what is needed to lift monoidal coalgebra modalities.

Proposition 9
.

Let
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
be a symmetric comonoidal monad and

(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
be a monoidal coalgebra modality on the same symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
. Then the following are in bijective correspondence:

(1)

Symmetric monoidal mixed distributive laws of
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
over
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
;

(2)

Liftings of the monoidal coalgebra modality
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
to
\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}}
, that is, a monoidal coalgebra modality
(\tilde{\oc},\tilde{\delta_{A}},\tilde{\varepsilon},\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\mathsf{e}},\tilde{\mathsf{m}},\tilde{\mathsf{m}}_{(K,\mathsf{n}_{K})})
on
(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}},\otimes^{\mathsf{n}},(K,\mathsf{n}_{K}))
which is a lifting of the underlying coalgebra modality
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
to
(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}},\otimes^{\mathsf{n}},(K,\mathsf{n}_{K}))
(in the sense of Proposition
7
) and a lifting of the underlying symmetric monoidal comonad
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
to
(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}},\otimes^{\mathsf{n}},(K,\mathsf{n}_{K}))
(in the sense of Proposition
3
);

(3)

Liftings of the symmetric comonoidal monad
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
to
(\mathbb{X}^{\oc},\otimes^{\mathsf{m}},(K,\mathsf{m}_{K}))
(in the sense of Proposition
3
).

Proof 6.4
.

That
(1)\Leftrightarrow(3)
is precisely part of Proposition
3
. While that
(1)\Leftrightarrow(2)
follows from Proposition
3
, Lemma
8
, and Proposition
7
. The bijective correspondence also follows from Proposition
3
and Proposition
7
.

We give a name to symmetric comonoidal monads with symmetric monoidal mixed distributive laws over monoidal coalgebra modalities:

{defi}

Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
be a monoidal coalgebra modality on a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
. An
exponential lifting monad
of
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
is a sextuple

(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K},\lambda)
consisting of a symmetric comonoidal monad
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
on
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
and a symmetric monoidal mixed distributive law
\lambda
of
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
over
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
.

Proposition
9
implies that the Eilenberg-Moore category of an exponential lifting monad admits a monoidal coalgebra modality which is strictly preserved by the forgetful functor. We can also extend on Proposition
4
for monoidal coalgebra modalities.

Proposition 10
.

Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
be a monoidal coalgebra modality on a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
. Then the following are in bijective correspondence:

(1)

Monoids in
(\mathbb{X}^{\oc},\otimes^{\mathsf{m}},(K,\mathsf{m}_{K}))
;

(2)

Cocommutative bimonoids
(A,\nabla,\mathsf{u},\Delta,\mathsf{e})
in
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
equipped with a natural transformation
\lambda_{X}:A\otimes\oc(X)\to\oc(A\otimes X)
such that
(A\otimes-,\mu^{\nabla},\eta^{\mathsf{u}},\mathsf{n}^{\Delta},\mathsf{n}^{\mathsf{e}}_{K},\lambda)
is an exponential lifting monad (where the symmetric comonoidal monad is defined as in Example
3.2
) of
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
and
\lambda
satisfies (
43
).

Therefore, each monoid in the Eilenberg-Moore category of a monoidal coalgebra modality induces an exponential lifting monad.

Proof 6.5
.

At first glance, it may be somewhat surprising that one only requires a monoid to obtain an exponential lifting monad while a cocommutative bimonoid was needed in Proposition
4
to construct a symmetric monoidal mixed distributive law. However since the Eilenberg-Moore category of a monoidal coalgebra modality is a Cartesian monoidal category it follows that every monoid is in fact a cocommutative bimonoid (Lemma
2.3
). Therefore the bijective correspondence follows immediately from Proposition
4
.

6.3.
Lifting Linear Category Structure

The last piece of the puzzle for lifting the linear category structure is being able to lift the symmetric monoidal closed structure strictly. This is easily achieved by required the exponential lifting monads to be symmetric Hopf monads.

{defi}

A
\mathsf{MELL}
lifting monad
on a linear category is an exponential lifting monad (with respect to the linear category’s monoidal coalgebra modality) whose underlying symmetric comonoidal monad is also a symmetric Hopf monad.

It is worth mentioning that in the definitions of an exponential lifting monad and of a
\mathsf{MELL}
lifting monad, we do not require that the underlying endofunctors of these monads be linearly distributive functors between linear categories in the sense of
[
HS03
,
Mel04
]
.

\mathsf{MELL}
lifting monads provide us with following main result of this paper:

Theorem 11
.

The Eilenberg-Moore category of a
\mathsf{MELL}
lifting monad is a linear category such that the forgetful functor preserves the linear category structure strictly.

Recalling that cocommutative Hopf monoids in a Cartesian monoidal category are known as groups (Definition
2.3
), then using Proposition
5
one can easily extend Proposition
10
to the context of linear categories.

Theorem 12
.

Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
be a monoidal coalgebra modality on a symmetric monoidal closed category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
, that is,
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
is a linear category. Then the following are in bijective correspondence:

(1)

Groups in
(\mathbb{X}^{\oc},\otimes^{\mathsf{m}},(K,\mathsf{m}_{K}))
;

(2)

Cocommutative Hopf monoids
(H,\nabla,\mathsf{u},\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{S})
in
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
equipped with a natural transformation
\lambda_{X}:H\otimes\oc(X)\to\oc(H\otimes X)
such that
(H\otimes-,\mu^{\nabla},\eta^{\mathsf{u}},\mathsf{n}^{\Delta},\mathsf{n}^{\mathsf{e}}_{K},\lambda)
is a
\mathsf{MELL}
lifting monad (where the symmetric Hopf monad is defined as in Example
3.3
),
\lambda
satisfies (
43
) and (
48
).

Therefore, for each group
((H,\omega),\nabla,\mathsf{u},\mathsf{S})
in
(\mathbb{X}^{\oc},\otimes^{\mathsf{m}},(K,\mathsf{m}_{K}))
,
\mathsf{MOD}(H,\nabla,\mathsf{u})
is a linear category.

Finally recall that the Eilenberg-Moore category of a free exponential modality is equivalent to the category of cocommutative comonoids on the base category, it follows that groups in this Eilenberg-Moore category are in bijective correspondence to cocommutative Hopf monoids in the base category. Therefore we can conclude the following for Lafont categories:

Corollary 13
.

If
(H,\nabla,\mathsf{u},\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{S})
is a cocommutative Hopf monoid in a Lafont category, then
\mathsf{MOD}(H,\nabla,\mathsf{u})
is a Lafont category.

7.
Mixed Distributive Laws for Differential Categories

In this final section, we discuss lifting differential category structure and how additive structure induces a source of exponential lifting monads and
\mathsf{MELL}
lifting monads.

7.1.
Additive Symmetric Monoidal Categories

Here we mean “additive” in the Blute, Cockett, and Seely sense of the term
[
BCS06
]
, that is, enriched over commutative monoids. In particular, we do not assume negatives (at least not yet… see Theorem
16
) nor do we assume biproducts which differs from other definitions of an additive category found in the literature
[
ML13
]
. Also in this section, we will often use equational representation instead of commutative diagrams as it is easier to work directly with equations when addition is involved. We will use diagrammatic order for composition: this means that the composite maps
f:A\to B
and
g:B\to C
is denoted
f;g:A\to C
, which is the map which first does
f
then
g
.

{defi}

An
additive category

[
BCS06
]
is a category
\mathbb{X}
such that for each pair of objects
A
and
B
, the hom-set
\mathbb{X}(A,B)
is a commutative monoid (in the classical sense) with binary operation
+:\mathbb{X}(A,B)\times\mathbb{X}(A,B)\to\mathbb{X}(A,B)
called
addition
and unit
0_{A,B}:A\to B
called the
zero map
, and furthermore that composition preserves the additive structure in the sense that:

k;(f+g);h=(k;f;h)+(k;g;h)\quad\quad\quad 0;f=0=f;0

An
additive symmetric monoidal category

[
BCS06
]
is a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
such that
\mathbb{X}
is an additive category and the monoidal product
\otimes
is compatible with the additive structure in the sense that:

(f+g)\otimes h=f\otimes h+g\otimes h\quad\quad\quad 0\otimes f=0

{exas}

Here are some examples of additive symmetric monoidal categories. Furthermore in these examples, the additive structures are induced from biproducts (see
[
ML13
]
for how biproducts induced an additive category structure).

(1)

The category of sets
\mathsf{SET}

cannot
be made into an additive category. If
\mathsf{SET}
was an additive category then categorical product (which is the Cartesian product) would coincide with the categorical coproduct (which is the disjoint union) – which is clearly not the case. Therefore
(\mathsf{SET},\times,\{\ast\})
is not an additive symmetric monoidal category.

(2)

On the other hand,
(\mathsf{REL},\times,\{\ast\})
is an additive symmetric monoidal where the sum of relations
R:X\to Y
and
S:X\to Y
, which recall are subsets
R,S\subseteq X\times Y
, is defined as their union
R+S:=R\cup S
, while the zero map
0_{X,Y}:X\to Y
is the empty relation
0_{X,Y}:=\emptyset
.

(3)

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a field. Then
(\mathsf{VEC}_{\mathbb{K}},\otimes,\mathbb{K})
is an additive symmetric monoidal category where the sum of
\mathbb{K}
-linear maps
f:V\to W
and
g:V\to W
is the standard sum of linear maps
f+g
defined pointwise,
(f+g)(v)=f(v)+g(v)
, and where the zero map
0_{V,W}:V\to W
is the
\mathbb{K}
-linear map which maps every element of
V
to the zero element of
W
.

It is worth mentioning that every additive category can be completed to a category with biproducts
[
ML13
]
(which is itself an additive category), and similarly every additive symmetric monoidal category can be completed to an additive symmetric monoidal category with distributive biproducts. For this reason, it is possible to argue
[
Fio07
]
that one should always assume a setting with biproducts. The problem is that arbitrary coalgebra modalities do not necessarily extend to the biproduct completion. However, monoidal coalgebra modalities induce monoidal coalgebra modalities on the biproduct completion (see
[
BCLS19
]
for more details).

7.2.
Exponential Lifting Monads from Additive Structure

In this section, we turn to monoidal coalgebra modalities over additive symmetric monoidal categories to provide us with a source of monoids in the Eilenberg-Moore category, which by Proposition
10
also provides a source of exponential lifting monads.

(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})

In fact, this natural (co)commutative bimonoid structure provides an equivalent definition of a monoidal coalgebra modality on an additive symmetric monoidal category. This equivalent definition is known as an
additive bialgebra modality

[
BCLS19
]
. We conclude this section with the observation that for a monoidal coalgebra modality on an additive symmetric monoidal category, every object induces an exponential lifting monad.

Proposition 14
.

Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
be a monoidal coalgebra on an additive symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
. Then for every object
A
,
(\oc(A)\otimes-,\mu^{\nabla_{A}},\eta^{\mathsf{u}_{A}},\mathsf{n}^{\Delta_{A}},\mathsf{n}^{\mathsf{e}_{K}},\delta_{A}^{\flat})
is an exponential lifting monad of
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
where the symmetric comonoidal monads is defined as in Example
3.2
and the symmetric monoidal mixed distributive law is defined as in (
46
).

Proof 7.1
.

This follows immediately from Proposition
10
.

7.3.
\mathsf{MELL}
Lifting Monads from Negatives

In this section we add negatives to the story to obtain a source of groups in the Eilenberg-Moore category of a monoidal coalgebra modality and therefore also a source of
\mathsf{MELL}
lifting monads. In particular, we will show that the cofree coalgebras of monoidal coalgebra modalities on additive symmetric monoidal categories are Hopf monoids precisely when the additive symmetric monoidal category also admits negatives. Here we mean “admits negatives” in the sense of being enriched over abelian groups.

{defi}

An additive category
\mathbb{X}
is said to have
negatives
if for each map admits an additive inverse, that is, for every map
f:A\to B
there is a map
-f:A\to B
such that
f+(-f)=0_{A,B}
.

We first note that for an additive symmetric monoidal category to have negatives: it is sufficient for the identity map of the monoidal unit to have an additive inverse.

Lemma 15
.

An additive symmetric monoidal category
(X,\otimes,K)
admits negatives if and only if the identity of the monoidal unit
1_{K}:K\to K
admits an additive inverse.

Proof 7.2
.

One direction is automatic by definition. For the converse, suppose that
1_{K}
admits an additive inverse
-1_{K}:K\to K
such that
1_{K}+(-1_{K})=0
. For a map
f:A\to B
, define the map
-f:A\to B
as follows:

-f:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.94438pt\raise 6.62279pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.61723pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ell^{-1}_{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 66.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{K\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 113.48073pt\raise 6.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(-1_{K})\otimes f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 161.68048pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 161.68048pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{K\otimes B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 219.5649pt\raise 5.93333pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.92778pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ell_{B}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 257.19775pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 257.19775pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Then by the additive symmetric monoidal structure, it follows that
f+(-f)=0
.

\displaystyle f+(-f)

\displaystyle=~(\ell^{-1}_{A};\ell_{A};f)+\left(\ell^{-1}_{A};((-1_{K})\otimes f);\ell_{B}\right)

\displaystyle=~(\ell^{-1}_{A};(1_{K}\otimes f);\ell_{B})+\left(\ell^{-1}_{A};((-1_{K})\otimes f);\ell_{B}\right)

(Nat. of
\ell
)

\displaystyle=~\ell^{-1}_{A};\left((1_{K}+(-1_{K})\otimes f\right);\ell_{B}

(Definition
7.1
)

\displaystyle=~\ell^{-1}_{A};\left(0_{K,K}\otimes f\right);\ell_{B}

\displaystyle=~\ell^{-1}_{A};0_{K\otimes A,K\otimes B};\ell_{B}

(Definition
7.1
)

\displaystyle=~0_{A,B}

(Definition
7.1
)

Furthermore, in an additive symmetric monoidal category which has negatives, the tensor product is compatible with negatives in the sense that
-(f\otimes g)=(-f)\otimes g=f\otimes(-g)
.

Proposition 16
.

Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
be a monoidal coalgebra modality on an additive symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
. Then there exists a natural transformation
\mathsf{S}_{A}:\oc(A)\to\oc(A)
such that for each object
A
, the septuple
(\oc(A),\nabla_{A},\mathsf{u}_{A},\Delta_{A},\mathsf{e}_{A},\mathsf{S}_{A})
is a cocommutative Hopf monoid (where
\nabla_{A}
and
\mathsf{u}_{A}
are defined as in (
53
)) if and only if the additive symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
is enriched over abelian groups.

Proof 7.3
.

\Leftarrow
: Suppose that
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
is enriched over abelian groups. Define the natural transformation
\mathsf{S}_{A}:\oc(A)\to\oc(A)
as
\mathsf{S}_{A}:=\oc(-1_{A})
. Since the bimonoid is (co)commutative
(\oc(A),\nabla_{A},\mathsf{u}_{A},\Delta_{A},\mathsf{e}_{A})
, we only need to check one half of (
13
) to show that

(\oc(A),\nabla_{A},\mathsf{u}_{A},\Delta_{A},\mathsf{e}_{A},\mathsf{S}_{A})
is a Hopf monoid. Then that
\mathsf{S}
is an antipode for

(\oc(A),\nabla_{A},\mathsf{u}_{A},\Delta_{A},\mathsf{e}_{A})
follows from commutativity of the following diagram:

\textstyle{\oc(A)\otimes\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{\oc(A)}\otimes\oc(-1_{A})}
\textstyle{\oc(A)\otimes\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nabla_{A}}
\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{e}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\oc(0_{A,A})}
\textstyle{\oc(A)}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\ref{addbialgdef})}
\scriptstyle{(\ref{addbialgdef})}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{u}_{A}}

Furthermore note that by definition,
\mathsf{S}_{A}:(\oc(A),\delta_{A})\to(\oc(A),\delta_{A})
is a
\oc
-coalgebra morphism.

\Rightarrow
: Suppose there exists a natural transformation
\mathsf{S}_{A}:\oc(A)\to\oc(A)
such that for each object
A
, the septuple
(\oc(A),\nabla_{A},\mathsf{u}_{A},\Delta_{A},\mathsf{e}_{A},\mathsf{S}_{A})
is a cocommutative Hopf monoid. By Lemma
15
it suffices to show that
1_{K}:K\to K
has an additive inverse. Define
-1_{K}:K\to K
as follows:

\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{m}_{K}}
\textstyle{\oc(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{S}_{K}}
\textstyle{\oc(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{K}}
\textstyle{K}

That
1_{K}+(-1_{K})=0_{K,K}
follows from the following equalities:

\displaystyle 0_{K,K}

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{u}_{K};\varepsilon_{K}

(
54
)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{m}_{K};\mathsf{e}_{K};\mathsf{u}_{K};\varepsilon_{K}

(
33
)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{m}_{K};\Delta_{K};(\mathsf{S}_{K}\otimes 1_{\oc(K)});\nabla_{K};\varepsilon_{K}

(
13
)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{m}_{K};\Delta_{K};(\mathsf{S}_{K}\otimes 1_{\oc(K)});\left[\left((\varepsilon_{K}\otimes\mathsf{e}_{K});\rho_{K}\right)+\left((\mathsf{e}_{K}\otimes\varepsilon_{K});\ell_{K}\right)\right]

(
54
)

\displaystyle=~\left[\mathsf{m}_{K};\Delta_{K};(\mathsf{S}_{K}\otimes 1_{\oc(K)});(\varepsilon_{K}\otimes\mathsf{e}_{K});\rho_{K}\right]+\left[\mathsf{m}_{K};\Delta_{K};(\mathsf{S}_{K}\otimes 1_{\oc(K)});(\mathsf{e}_{K}\otimes\varepsilon_{K});\ell_{K}\right]

(Definition
7.1
)

\displaystyle=~\left[\mathsf{m}_{K};\Delta_{K};(\mathsf{S}_{K}\otimes 1_{\oc(K)});(\varepsilon_{K}\otimes\mathsf{e}_{K});\rho_{K}\right]+\left[\mathsf{m}_{K};\Delta_{K};(\mathsf{e}_{K}\otimes\varepsilon_{K});\ell_{K}\right]

(Lemma
2.3
(ii))

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathsf{m}_{K};\mathsf{S}_{K};\varepsilon_{K}\right)+\left(\mathsf{m}_{K};\varepsilon_{K}\right)

(
8
)

\displaystyle=~(-1_{K})+1_{K}

\displaystyle=~1_{K}+(-1_{K})

Therefore we obtain the following:

Theorem 17
.

Let
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
be a linear category with monoidal coalgebra modality

(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
such that
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
is also an additive symmetric monoidal category with negatives. Then for every object
A
,
(\oc(A)\otimes-,\mu^{\nabla_{A}},\eta^{\mathsf{u}_{A}},\mathsf{n}^{\Delta_{A}},\mathsf{n}^{\mathsf{e}_{K}},\delta_{A}^{\flat})
is a
\mathsf{MELL}
lifting monad and therefore
\left(\mathsf{MOD}(\oc(A),\nabla_{A},\mathsf{u}_{A}),\otimes^{\mathsf{n}^{\Delta_{A}}},(K,\mathsf{n}^{\mathsf{e}_{A}}_{K})\right)
is a linear category.

Proof 7.4
.

This theorem follows directly from Proposition
16
and Theorem
12
.

7.4.
Lifting Differential Category Structure

We briefly give the definition of a differential category. For more details on differential categories see
[
BCS06
,
BCS15
,
BCLS19
]
.

{defi}

A differential category
[
BCS06
]
is an additive symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
with a coalgebra
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
equipped with a deriving transformation, that is, a natural transformation
\mathsf{d}_{A}:\oc(A)\otimes A\to\oc(A)
satisfying the identities found in
[
BCS06
, Definition 2.5]
.

In order to be able to lift differential category structure, we will need that the Eilenberg-Moore category of our symmetric comonoidal monad is an additive symmetric monoidal category. In order to achieve this, we will need that the underlying endofunctor preserves the additive structure.

{defi}

An
additive functor
between additive categories is a functor which preserves the additive structure strictly, that is, a functor
\mathsf{T}
such that
\mathsf{T}(f+g)=\mathsf{T}(f)+\mathsf{T}(g)
and
\mathsf{T}(0)=0
.

One can easily check that for a monad on an additive category whose underlying endofunctor is additive, that its Eilenberg-Moore category is also an additive category such that the forgetful functor preserves the additive structure strictly. Similarly, for a symmetric comonoidal monad on an additive symmetric monoidal category whose underlying endofunctor is additive, its Eilenberg-Moore category is also an additive category such that the forgetful functor preserves the additive symmetric monoidal structure strictly. Luckily, for any additive symmetric monoidal category our favourite endofunctor
A\otimes-
is additive for any object
A
.

{defi}

Let
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
be a differential category with coalgebra modality

(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
equipped with deriving transformation
\mathsf{d}
, and let
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
be a symmetric comonoidal monad on
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
whose underlying endofunctor
\mathsf{T}
is additive. A
differential mixed distributive law of
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
over
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{d})
is a coalgebra mixed distributive law
\lambda
of
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
over
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
such that the following diagram commutes:

(57)

Proposition 18
.

Let
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
be a differential category with coalgebra modality

(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
equipped with deriving transformation
\mathsf{d}
, and let
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
be a symmetric comonoidal monad on
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
whose underlying endofunctor is additive. Then the following are in bijective correspondence:

(1)

Differential mixed distributive laws
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
over
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{d})
;

(2)

Liftings of
\mathsf{d}
to
(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}},\otimes^{\mathsf{n}},(K,\mathsf{n}_{K}))
, that is, a deriving transformation
\tilde{\mathsf{d}}
for the induced lifted coalgebra modality
(\tilde{\oc},\tilde{\delta},\tilde{\varepsilon},\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\mathsf{e}})
on
(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}},\otimes^{\mathsf{n}},(K,\mathsf{n}_{K}))
from Proposition
7
such that for every
\oc
-colagbera
(A,\omega)
,
\tilde{\mathsf{d}}_{(A,\omega)}=\mathsf{d}_{A}
.

Therefore if
\lambda
is a differential mixed distributive law of
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
over
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{d})
, then
(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}},\otimes^{\mathsf{n}},(K,\mathsf{n}_{K}))
is a differential category with coalgebra modality
(\tilde{\oc},\tilde{\delta},\tilde{\varepsilon},\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\mathsf{e}})
and deriving transformation
\tilde{\mathsf{d}}
.

Proof 7.5
.

The bijective correspondence will follow immediately from Proposition
7
. Therefore, it remains to show that we can obtain one from the other.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
: Let
\lambda
be a differential mixed distributive law of
(\mathsf{T},\mu,\eta,\mathsf{n},\mathsf{n}_{K})
over
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{d})
. Consider the induced lifting of
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
from Proposition
7
. To prove that we have a lifting of the deriving transformation it suffices to show that
\mathsf{d}
is a
\mathsf{T}
-algebra morphism. Then if
(A,\nu)
is a
\mathsf{T}
-algebra, commutativity of the following diagram shows that
\mathsf{d}_{A}
is a
\mathsf{T}
-algebra morphism:

\textstyle{\mathsf{T}(\oc(A)\otimes A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\ref{distderive})}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{T}(\mathsf{d}_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{n}_{\oc(A),A}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{T}\oc(A)\otimes\mathsf{T}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{A}\otimes 1_{\mathsf{T}(A)}}
\textstyle{\oc\mathsf{T}(A)\otimes\mathsf{T}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{T}(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\oc(\nu)\otimes\nu}
\scriptstyle{\text{Nat. of. }\mathsf{d}_{A}}
\textstyle{\oc(A)\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{T}\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{A}}
\textstyle{\oc\mathsf{T}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\oc(\nu)}
\textstyle{\oc(A)}

is a

-algebra morphism which in particular for free

where
\mathsf{n}^{\Delta_{A}}_{\oc(B)\otimes B}
is defined as in (
23
). This coherence allows one to easily lift differential category structure to categories of modules.

Proposition 19
.

Let
(\mathbb{X},\otimes,K)
be a differential category with a monoidal coalgebra modality
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{K})
equipped with deriving transformation
\mathsf{d}
. Then for every monoid
((A,\omega),\nabla,\mathsf{u})
in
(\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{T}},\otimes^{\mathsf{n}},(K,\mathsf{n}_{K}))
, the induced symmetric monoidal mixed distributive law from Proposition
10
satisfies (
57
) and therefore the category of modules over
(A,\nabla,\mathsf{u})
is a differential category.

8.
Conclusion

The main goal of this paper was to study for which monoids in a linear category was the category of modules of said monoid again a linear category. We showed that the answer to this question was groups in the Eilenberg-Moore category of the monoidal coalgebra modality of a linear category, that is, cocommutative Hopf monoids in the linear category which come equipped with a special natural transformation (Theorem
12
). Therefore categories of modules over these cocommutative Hopf monoids are linear categories and therefore a model of
\mathsf{MELL}
. Thus the work of this paper extends Blute and Scott’s work
[
BS04
,
Blu96
]
by adding to the study of categories of modules over Hopf monoids as models of linear logic. This work should also hopefully pave the way for studying other applications of Hopf monoids in linear logic. For example, the bimonoid version of a coalgebra modality is known as a bialgebra modality, which is used in the study of differential categories
[
BCS06
,
BCLS19
]
. As such one could study the notion of a “Hopf algebra modality”, a Hopf monoid version of a coalgebra modality, and look at what the antipode adds to the story. In particular, Proposition
16
implies that a monoidal coalgebra modality over an additive symmetric monoidal category with negatives should be an example of a “Hopf algebra modality”.

This paper also introduces the notion of a symmetric monoidal mixed distributive law (Definition
5.2
) and showed these are in bijective correspondence with liftings of symmetric monoidal comonads to the Eilenberg-Moore category of symmetric comonoidal monads and vice-versa (Proposition
3
). While in this paper we focused mainly on symmetric comonoidal monads of the form
A\otimes-
, it would be interesting to find and study more exotic examples of symmetric monoidal mixed distributive laws (whether they are related to
\mathsf{MELL}
or not). One other possible direction would be to study symmetric monoidal mixed distributive laws from a higher category theory perspective.
